# Zinc Next: decisions after M1 to M6 (ZN-031)

Recorded 2026-10-06 from the results of the proof of concept. They close the "Decisions still open" of `zinc-next-design.md` (section 10).

## 1. Name of the desktop app: **Zinc Atelier**

`ZincStudio` already names the macOS box editor, so the app needs another name. Criteria: short, says "a place where you make things" (editor,
devices, profiler in one app), not a generic word that is already a famous developer tool. "Forge" (Electron Forge, Minecraft Forge) and "Foundry"
(Palantir, Foundry VTT) were set aside for that reason. The command line keeps the name `zinc`; the app is **Zinc Atelier**.
Checked: no npm package and no GitHub repository of the closed spellings of the candidates. Not checked: trademarks and domains (task ZN-056).
Fallbacks if it is taken: Zinc Forge, Zinc Foundry.

## 2. Shell of the app: a Zinc app (zinc:ui), not a web shell

The app is written in Zinc on `zinc:ui` and runs on the engine like any other app (the end goal in the product vision: one self-contained
program for Windows, macOS and Linux; no Tauri, no Electron). The proof of concept shows the base is there: the whole `zinc:ui` stack
(Solid signals, kit components, layout, text) runs in the interpreter and in AOT and draws pixel-identical frames (ZN-044, ZN-045).
What is missing is planned as tasks ZN-047 to ZN-055: a live window and input, fonts and images baked by the engine, the host modules
apps need, the shell itself, the QuickJS path, a stable IR, packaging, Windows and Linux hosts, and hardware validation.

## 3. QuickJS: the full engine, as a second engine

QuickJS is kept as a full engine next to the interpreter and the AOT output, not only for `zinc:script`: it runs plain JavaScript (npm code,
`eval`, dynamic plugins) that the typed subset does not cover, behind the same host ABI. The typed engine stays the default and the fast path
(the interpreter is 3 to 6 times faster than QuickJS on the numeric kernels, ZN-026). Constrained targets are evaluated separately (ZN-043, mquickjs).
Task ZN-051.

## 4. `--emit=ir`: not stable now, and it must become stable

The IR text changed with almost every task of M3 to M6 (inlining, devirtualisation, closures, host calls), and its goldens are regenerated each
time. It stays unstable until the passes settle. The aim is a stable, versioned format: a spec, a version number in the dump, a parser that
reads it back, and a compatibility test over old dumps. Task ZN-052. Until then nothing outside the repository may depend on the text.

## Also settled

- `clang` is not replaced by an in-process backend for now: the pinned `zig c++` is downloaded, checked and used in about 20 s (ZN-029) and
  cross builds for aarch64, armhf and x86_64 work. Revisit if the toolchain size or the first-build time proves a problem for users.
- The default path for devices is bytecode upload to a preflashed core (ZN-030); AOT is the path for speed.

## 5. Name check for Zinc Atelier (ZN-056, 2026-10-06)

What was searched, and what came back (a web search and DNS and registry look-ups; no trademark office was queried, see the limits):

| Where | Result |
|---|---|
| Web search "Zinc Atelier" (software, app, trademark) | no product, company or mark of that name |
| Marks containing ZINC (USPTO listings found by the search) | several registrations of the single word ZINC for software and apps (TemTree Co., Senseeker Engineering, Silver-Katz Entertainment, Summum S.A.) and a mark of Zinc Labs Inc (OpenObserve); older products named Zinc: the Zinc Application Framework (a C++ GUI toolkit), Zinc Inc. (messaging) |
| Domains | `zincatelier.com` is registered (Cloudflare name servers, no site); `zincatelier.io` is not registered; `zincatelier.dev` and `.app` have no DNS records (probably free, to be confirmed at a registrar); `zincforge.com` is registered (Porkbun); `zincforge.dev` and `zincfoundry.com` have no records |
| Registries | `zinc-atelier` and `zincatelier` are free on npm, PyPI and crates.io |
| GitHub | no repository named zinc atelier |

**Decision.** Keep **Zinc Atelier** as the name of the app. No product carries it and the registries are free. The risk is the word ZINC, not Atelier: other owners hold ZINC marks in software classes, so
the name is used descriptively as "Zinc Atelier, the app of the Zinc language" and not registered as a mark by us until a lawyer has cleared it. Domain: register `zincatelier.dev` (or `.app`) first; `.com` is taken. Fallback if a
conflict appears: **Zinc Foundry** (`zincfoundry.com` looks free), not Zinc Forge (`zincforge.com` is taken). The legacy ZincStudio box editor keeps its name; the two do not coexist in one package
(Atelier is the app of the new engine, ZincStudio belongs to the old compiler).

Limits: a trademark office search (USPTO, EUIPO, WIPO) and a registrar's availability check are not scriptable from here; the table is evidence for the decision, not legal advice.

## 6. MicroQuickJS (ZN-043, 2026-10-06)

Measured (`zinc-next-mquickjs.md`): about as fast as QuickJS, 4 to 7 times slower than the Zinc interpreter; 103 KB of Thumb-2 code against 125 KB for the Zinc core; 8 KB of heap for fib against a 24 KB register file, and 4 bytes per number in an array
against 8. It runs ES5 strict mode only, so the ES2022 path of ZN-051 does not carry over. **Decision: not adopted, not vendored.** Revisit for a product that needs user JavaScript on a device with under 100 KB of free RAM; then as a fallback engine behind `zinc:script`.

## 7. Parity plan decisions (2026-10-07)

Made under `RULES.md` from the four parity audits in `docs/reports/parity/` (01 language and standard library, 02 examples, 03 plugins and targets, 04 library choices). Scores follow RULES.md section 4; the reasoning and the evidence are in the audit files.

| # | Question | Decision | Why in one line | Revisit when |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D1 | Native-module ABI | Evolve `runtime/include/zinc_abi.h` v4 into `include/zn/native.h`: a typed export table with the signature letters of `runtime.h`, bound by name at load, one `CallNative` opcode, versioning and finalizers from Node-API, own/borrow rules from WIT. libffi only for `zinc:ffi` | Exists and is tested on two engines; plugin C++ links unchanged through a `zrt` marshalling layer; no rewrite of ~12 000 lines | An ABI v2 is needed by a plugin that cannot express its API |
| D2 | Plugins: shared library or linked in | Desktop interpreter: one shared library per plugin, built and cached by the pinned zig; AOT programs and firmware: static link, registered by generated code | Small `zinc`, hot reload, cache; static where there is no dynamic loader | A target without `dlopen` needs the desktop path |
| D3 | Prototype plugin C++ | Compatibility adapters (`src/native/zrt_compat`) keep the 22 native specs unchanged; new native code uses the C ABI | Same behaviour at once, no churn | The adapters cost more than 10% on a plugin benchmark |
| D4 | PS2 | Build-only gate; the PCSX2 runner runs only when the owner supplies a BIOS path | An emulator cannot run without a BIOS we may not ship | The owner supplies a BIOS |
| D5 | PS1 | Spike first (a freestanding runtime and fixed-point lowering), the target afterwards; not on the critical path | Largest and most uncertain target | The spike's verdict |
| D6 | Raspberry Pi | `aarch64-linux` is the primary Pi target (qemu-user as the gate); armv6 (`armhf`) secondary | Matches `raspberry-pi.md`; 64-bit Pi OS is the shipped OS | A Pi 1 product need |
| D7 | Sockets and event loop | libuv on desktop and Pi, llhttp plus own glue for HTTP, wslay for WebSocket, mbedTLS for TLS and crypto; mongoose and wolfSSL rejected for licence | Mature, MIT/Apache, portable (also what a later Windows port needs) | Footprint on small targets |
| D8 | SVG and Lottie | ThorVG for both on desktop-class targets; nanosvg stays for the tiny profile. Goldens are re-baked in their own commits with a tolerance, the shared rasterizer remains the reference for shapes | Proven, maintained, one library for two formats instead of 1800 custom lines | A pixel regression the tolerance cannot absorb |
| D9 | Display simulation depth | Level 1 (window plus frame hashes) for all, Level 2 (chip models for SSD1306, ST7789, WS2812, IS31FL3730, QMI8658 behind a bus shim) for the shipped boards | "Validated by simulator" must mean the bus bytes were checked | Chip models prove too costly for a panel |
| D10 | Regex, Unicode, JSON, numbers | QuickJS-ng libregexp/libunicode (split files), libunibreak, yyjson, fast_float, dragonbox | Same regex semantics as the JS engine, fastest permissive options | A measurement says otherwise (each adopting task measures) |
| D11 | Fonts, images, rendering | stb_truetype plus a desktop HarfBuzz/SheenBidi tier; stb_image/stb_image_write, libwebp as plugin; software rasterizer is the reference and GLAD GL 3.3/GLES3 a second path; SDL3 vendored statically | Keeps pixels stable and sizes small; GPU is an addition | GPU path drift in goldens |
| D12 | Media | VideoToolbox/V4L2 first, dav1d and openh264 as run-time plugins, miniaudio/minimp3 for audio; FFmpeg only as a dynamically loaded plugin (LGPL) | Licence safety | A format gap |
| D13 | JIT | No JIT now. If measured need appears, a sljit baseline JIT from ZBC. The AOT and the typed interpreter already meet the performance gates; the prototype's AArch64 JIT is not ported | Complexity and security surface for no measured gain | Interpreter needs another 2x on a target without a compiler JavaScript libraries on QuickJS are a separate question: D44. |
| D14 | Fast-math AOT (ZN-042) | No fast-math: bit-identical rounding across engines is a product rule. nbody stays about 2.9x native; vectorization without FMA contraction is a separate optimisation task | Determinism over a benchmark | Owner asks |
| D15 | Corpora and fuzzing | test262 subset, WPT URL/encoding data, wasm testsuite, JSONTestSuite as pinned data; libFuzzer with ASan/UBSan on the parser and the ZBC verifier | Reuse existing oracles | none |

| D16 | Nullable scalars (ZN-065) | `T \| undefined` for number and boolean is the existing boxed `T \| null` representation (undefined reads as null). `Map.get` and `find`/`at` of scalars return it through generated lookups; truthiness of a nullable number or boolean uses a generated test | No new IR slot kind; reuses the Dyn-era box and its compare | Measured cost of the boxes on a hot path |
| D17 | Narrowing of property paths (ZN-066) | Paths of locals, `this`, fields and constant or local indexes (`this.head`, `n.next.next`, `a[i]`) narrow like variables. A write to a local, field or element forgets the paths below it; a call `p.m()` forgets the paths below `p`; a loop forgets what it writes or calls on. Calls of free functions forget nothing (as tsc); `m.get(k)` is not a path | Sound for the common aliasing, no whole-program analysis, close to tsc so demos keep compiling | A demo needs call-through writes via a free function |
| D18 | undefined versus null (ZN-074) | The two share one representation (a null reference, or a null box for scalars). The distinction is in the type: a union with null carries an `undef` flavour (optional fields and parameters, `T \| undefined`, `Map.get`, find, at, `a?.b`, a variable declared without a value) and the code generated for printing, templates, typeof and JSON follows it; a union that mixes a plain null in is a null one. Files that use Dyn values keep the Dyn undefined | No change to values, so the fast path and the IR are untouched (bench-m4 unchanged); a value that is null in one place and undefined in another prints by its static type, not by its history | A program that stores both into one variable and prints it |
| D19 | JSON and number formatting stay in tree (ZN-092, revises D10) | The runtime keeps its own JSON.parse into Dyn (a strict recursive parser that shares keys, short strings and small numbers) and `std::to_chars` for number printing; yyjson, fast_float and dragonbox are not added to the runtime. Scored 1-5 by speed / size / risk: keep 4/5/5, yyjson then convert 3/3/3 (a second tree and a second pass to build the Dyn), fast_float and dragonbox 3/4/4 (the C++ library already gives shortest round-trip digits, the ECMAScript layout is ours) | Measured first: JSONTestSuite 95 y_ accepted, 188 n_ rejected, the 35 i_ as Node (tests/t1/jsontestsuite.sh); String(number) hash of 1 000 000 doubles of every magnitude equals Node (tests/golden/run/number_print.ts). The one slow part was JSON.stringify of a Dyn tree, written in Zinc: now a runtime call (bench/json.ts: parse+stringify x10 1373 ms to 227 ms; QuickJS 690 ms). No new library, no size change | A platform whose libc++ lacks `std::to_chars` for double (the ESP32 firmware has its own printing); a document nested deeper than 1000 levels (Node accepts more) |
| D20 | `// @ts-ignore` and `// @ts-expect-error` (ZN-094) | Not honoured: a comment never hides a diagnostic. Scored by predictability / parity / effort: refuse 5/4/5, honour 3/3/2 | One rule for every file; the registry says what is wrong and the fix is in the code (a cast, an annotation, the construct). The prototype honoured them only through tsc, which Next does not run | A migrating project with ts-ignore lines gets new errors, listed in docs/diagnostics-vs-tsc.md |
| D21 | Native code versus stand-ins (ZN-101, refines D2) | `ZINC_NATIVE=auto` (default): a stand-in `x.next.ts` / `x.sim.ts` next to the spec wins when there is one, native code is built when there is none; `real` forces the plugin's native code (an error naming the cause when it cannot be built), `sim` is today's behaviour. The shared library exports `zn_module_<Name>` (one module per plugin spec) instead of one `zn_module_open`. Scored by parity / determinism / effort: auto 5/5/5, real-first 3/2/3 | The 49 demos that run today keep their stand-ins; golden tests stay deterministic and need no compiler; a plugin without a stand-in becomes usable at once | `real` by default once every plugin builds in CI and a stand-in is only a fallback |
| D22 | zinc:script on the engine's QuickJS-ng (ZN-103, applies D10) | A built-in native module `QuickJS` (src/qjs/script_native.cpp, the C ABI, registered by `zinc` and linked into AOT programs that use it) implements plugins/script/native/quickjs.spec.ts. `unknown` crosses as JSON text (generated wrappers `__nativeJson` / `__nativeValue` / `__nativeArgs`), a script function as `{"__zn_fn": n}` (a handle into the Vm's table, sent back it becomes the function). The plugin's own vendored QuickJS and its zrt::Dyn host.cpp are not used by Next. Scored by one-engine / parity / effort: this 5/4/3, the plugin's vendored copy through the thunk 2/5/4 (two QuickJS in one process, symbol clashes under -rdynamic) | One QuickJS, one regexp and unicode, no zrt in the script path; the prototype keeps its own host.cpp | Values whose JSON form loses information (NaN, undefined inside arrays, cycles) differ from the prototype's Dyn snapshot |
| D23 | SVG and Lottie renderers (ZN-106, revises D8) | Keep the prototype's own renderers (plugins/svg/native/svg.host.cpp, plugins/lottie/native/lottie.host.cpp): they already run unchanged through the plugin pipeline (thunk + cache + dlopen/static link) on the host library's rasterizer and are the parity baseline of every demo. ThorVG stays a later quality upgrade (more of the SVG and Lottie specs, expressions), to be weighed against a pixel-diff budget. Scored by parity / effort / coverage: keep 5/5/3, ThorVG 3/2/5 | No pixel re-bake and no 1 MB dependency for the same demos; the galleries draw real content now; the `.next.ts` metadata-only stand-in of lottie is removed | A demo needs a Lottie or SVG feature the custom renderers lack (then vendor ThorVG behind the same Spec) |
| D24 | Vector tiles (ZN-108, revises D11 for the map) | Keep the prototype's map engine (plugins/map/native/map.host.cpp: its own MVT decoder, triangulation and MapLibre-subset style evaluator): it already runs unchanged through the plugin pipeline and renders both map examples. protozero, vtzero and earcut.hpp stay a later upgrade if decoding cost (about 90 ms per new tile) matters on small targets. Scored by parity / effort / speed: keep 5/5/3, rewrite on protozero+vtzero+earcut 3/1/4 | No new vendored code and no pixel re-bake; the map draws real tiles | Pan cost on the Pi or a demand for more of the style spec |
| D25 | glTF reading (ZN-119, revises D11 for the 3D plugins) | Keep the three plugin's own reader (plugins/three/native/three.host.cpp, 489 lines, with the Zinc GLTFLoader on top): it runs unchanged through the plugin pipeline and renders both example models like the prototype. cgltf 1.15 and meshoptimizer 1.3 are vendored as an independent reader and as optimiser libraries: a T0 test diffs meshes, triangles, vertices and the world bounding box between cgltf and the loader. Scored by parity / effort / risk: keep 5/5/5, move the plugin onto cgltf 3/2/3 | Two readers agree on both models; meshoptimizer is there when a profile wants vertex-cache or quantised meshes | A model the prototype's reader rejects or misreads (the test list grows with it) |
| D26 | Target profiles on the host (ZN-120) | `--profile <name>` and zinc.json `profile` choose the table of the prototype (macos, linux, sim, wasm, rpi1, rmpp: f64; esp32, ps2: f32; ps1: fx12). The checker's `number` alias (`setNumberAlias`) and the lowering's default literal follow it; `number[]` meets the f64 host calls through a generated copy (`arrayConversion`); `**` on non-f64 goes through f64; the heap budget (`setHeapBudget`, global new/delete and object memory counted by usable size, 1 MiB headroom for the interpreter) stops the program with `out of memory (heap budget N bytes, asked A, in use U)`. `run` keeps gradual typing so the .f32 goldens print (they use `any`); a `build` under a strict profile is strict, and the library (zinc:*, lib/std, plugins) is exempt from strict. Scored by parity / effort / risk: this alias 5/4/4, a separate compile of the prelude per profile 4/1/3 | The 22 f32 conformance programs print their goldens; the allocator counts only when asked (one flag test per allocation) | fx12 (the Dyn prelude mixes f64 and fx12), plugin out-arrays under f32 (three), AOT builds with a budget |
| D27 | System integration spikes S1, S4, S5, S10 on macOS (ZN-230) | **S1 yes**: the engine copied into `~/.zinc/cache/macos/devapp/<app.id>.app` (8 MB, a copy takes milliseconds) with an Info.plist and an ad-hoc signature (`codesign --force --sign - --identifier <app.id>`, `codesign -v`: valid on disk) has a bundle id and display name (`lsappinfo`: LSDisplayName "Spike One"), and `UNUserNotificationCenter` answers there (`getNotificationSettings`: authorizationStatus 0 = not determined, no prompt); outside a bundle `mainBundle.bundleIdentifier` is nil. A **hard link does not work**: codesign replaces the file, the link count drops to 1 (copy, never link). Showing the permission prompt and a delivered-notification readback need one human click and were not run: dev falls back to `osascript` until the owner allows once; real notifications are certain only in `--bundle` builds. **S5 yes for URLs**: an `NSAppleEventManager` handler for `kAEGetURL` registered before `finishLaunching` receives the URL on a cold start (`open zspike://cold/start?x=1` launched the app and delivered it) and on a running app; LaunchServices registers the bundle only at the devapp cache path (a bundle under /private/tmp is not found: `lsregister -f` after each refresh), so the handler needs no delegate wrapping; the dock menu stays `class_addMethod` on SDL's delegate class (not measurable unattended). **S4 not measurable unattended** (a file panel waits for a click): decision NSOpenPanel directly in `system_macos.mm`, which also gives the sheet attachment. **S10 yes**: `zinc native-gen` accepts the three-function spec `supports(): string`, `call(op, json): string`, `poll(): string` (and a `onEvent(cb)` callback as `process` has); a Promise return is not expressible (Z5010), so async operations return a request id and finish with an event | Measured with a 60-line Objective-C++ program and the real `native-gen`; what needs a click or a screenshot is recorded as such, not guessed | Evidence for tray, dock and menu title screenshots, and the delivered-notification readback, when the owner runs `ZINC_SYSTEM_SELFTEST` once on the Mac |
| D28 | Spike S1: order ranks (ZN-173) | **Ranks do not pay on real Zinc frames; the kill criterion (draw calls <= 40% of kind-break batching) is not met.** `tools/rank-study` on 6 frames (hero 3, 8, 12; navigation 3, 8, 12): painter-order batching already needs only 36 to 84 batches for 1045 to 1342 primitives (the recorder emits shapes grouped by kind); ranked batching needs 49 to 55 (hero 65% to 88% of painter, navigation 136%, 302 vs 323 in total). Decision: the GPU backends keep painter-order batching with lookback merge (architecture section 4.x rule 4) as the default; the rank tree (ZN-177) and the rank-driven batch planner (ZN-178) become optional T3+ work behind a measured need, not a prerequisite. The CPU-cost half of S1 (5k prims < 0.5 ms on the Pi) is moot. Report: `docs/reports/render-rank-study.md` | Scored by evidence: ranks 1/5 on this corpus, painter order 4/5 | A scene with deep overlap (many small interleaved kinds) in the benchmark corpus |
| D29 | GL floor (ZN-176, amends D11 and ZN-116) | **The floor is GLES2 / GLSL ES 1.00 (T2: Pi 1/2/3, WebGL1); GLES3 / GLSL ES 3.00 (T3: Pi 4/5, WebGL2) and desktop GL 3.3 core (T4) are paths of the same code, not the baseline.** D11's "GL 3.3 / GLES3 via GLAD" is read as "desktop GL 3.3 core and GLES3 are supported", not as the minimum. One GLSL source per shader with a generated prelude: `#version 100` (ES2, `#define ZN_ES2`), `#version 300 es` (ES3, `ZN_ES3`) or `#version 330 core` (`ZN_GL33`), and macros for `attribute/in`, `varying/out`, `texture2D/texture`, `gl_FragColor/fragColor`. Features above the floor are probed by proc/extension availability (instancing: `glVertexAttribDivisor`, else `EXT_instanced_arrays`, else expanded quads), never by version. Matches the owner's goal of full WebGL compliance from Pi 3 (WebGL1) to desktop (WebGL2) | Scored by reach: GLES2 floor 5/5 (the only API on the Pi 1/2/3 and the one the implemented renderer and the Pi validation use), GLES3 floor 2/5 (drops every Pi before the 4), per-version shader copies 1/5 (drift) | A Pi-class device needing GLES3-only features on every frame |
| D30 | Spike S6: native T4 backend (ZN-199) | **No sokol_gfx and no Dawn. Raw GL / GLES through the D29 prelude is the baseline on every target; ANGLE (Metal backend) is an optional, separately built `libEGL` + `libGLESv2` pair for macOS only, behind the existing GL renderer, because Apple froze its GL at 4.1.** Scores (1-5, sum of 8 criteria): ANGLE 29, sokol_gfx 26, Dawn 19. sokol: Zlib, one header, seconds to build, but GLES2 is not a target, sokol-shdc wants GLSL 450 with tags (replaces D29's single-source prelude) and it is not a GL drop-in, so three.js and WebGL1/2 would need a rebuilt GL state machine. Dawn: WGSL only (rewrite of every shader), needs ES 3.1 or Vulkan so Pi 3 is out, 15+ pinned repos, tens of minutes of build, 8 to 15 MB. ANGLE: BSD-3, takes GLSL ES 1.00/3.00 as is, is the WebGL-conformant implementation that Chrome and Firefox use (matches the owner's full WebGL goal), 8 to 11 MB per dylib on macOS, gn + depot_tools build with no official CMake (community zig/vcpkg builds exist). Linux and Pi 4/5 use Mesa GL/GLES directly, no ANGLE. **Follow-up before adopting ANGLE-Metal:** measure the macOS gn build time, a pinned prebuilt dylib size and the licence bundling; if too heavy, macOS ships native GL 4.1 with a core-profile prelude. Numbers not fetched (licence files, line counts, build times) are marked unverified in the agent's report; the build-time claims are not measurements | Single-source shaders 5/2/1 (ANGLE/sokol/Dawn), WebGL 5/1/1, build cost 2/5/1 | The owner drops Pi 3 or WebGL parity (sokol becomes viable), or the ANGLE spike shows an unacceptable build or size cost |
| D31 | File watching for `zinc dev` (ZN-141, revises the efsw plan) | **Polling**: the project tree (without build, dist, node_modules, .git) is stat-ed every 40 ms and the settled state (30 ms later) triggers a check and a restart; no efsw. A project has tens to a few hundred files, a pass costs well under a millisecond of CPU, it is the same code on macOS, Linux and the Pi, and the measured save to new process time is about 55 ms (the interpreter starts in milliseconds), far below the 2 s gate. The prototype does not keep module state across reloads either, so restarting the process loses nothing the prototype offers. Scores: polling reach 5 (no vendored library, no inotify/FSEvents/kqueue split), efsw latency 5 against 4, efsw cost 2 (a vendored C++ library with three backends) against 5 | A project with tens of thousands of files, or a battery-bound laptop where the 40 ms wake-ups matter: then efsw (MIT) behind the same `snapshot()` seam |
| D32 | Shaped text per project (ZN-224) | **Opt-in: `zinc.json` `"text": "shaped"` (or `ZINC_TEXT=shaped` for `zinc run`).** Text with combining marks, Hebrew to Indic and Southeast Asian scripts, ZWJ, Arabic forms or emoji goes through `src/text/layout.h` over the embedded TTFs (the project's `assets/*.ttf` are the fallback chain), glyph bitmaps cached by gid; Latin, symbols and CJK keep the codepoint tables, so all examples stay pixel-identical with it on. Default-on for desktop scored 3 against 5 (no visible gain for Latin, 1.5 MB of shaping code in every program); a run-time loaded plugin 2. `zinc build` links the tier only on request (+1.5 MB). Cross builds do not link it yet. |
| D33 | Fonts beyond TrueType: CFF, system fallback, colour emoji (ZN-225) | **No FreeType.** stb_truetype reads CFF (OpenType `OTTO`) outlines, HarfBuzz shapes any of them, so FreeType (a 1 MB+ dependency with its own rasterizer, two rasterizers to keep identical) is not worth it: scored 2 against 5. The resource baker now accepts CFF fonts (embedded, no glyphs baked) and a baked font without glyphs always goes through the shaping tier. **System fonts policy:** a program that asks for shaped text gets, behind its own embedded fonts, a short per-platform list of system files opened the first time a code point is not covered or an emoji presentation selector (U+FE0F) appears: macOS `Apple Color Emoji.ttc`, `Apple Symbols.ttf`, `Arial Unicode.ttf`; Linux DejaVu Sans. No fontconfig/CoreText enumeration (no dependency, no scan cost); an app that needs a face ships it in `assets/`. Tiny profiles link nothing of this. **Colour:** `sbix` bitmaps (PNG strikes decoded with stb_image, box-scaled) are drawn; COLR/CPAL and CBDT (Noto Color Emoji on Linux) and variable-font instances are not yet. |
| D34 | WebP and JPEG decoding speed (ZN-226) | **libwebp 1.5.0 vendored** (decoder and encoder, BSD-3): `.webp` assets bake like PNGs, `ZINC_SHOT=*.webp` and `zinc capture --format webp` write lossless WebP. **libjpeg-turbo is not linked: stb_image stays the JPEG decoder.** Measured (this Mac, M-series, `-O2`, 4032x3024 quality-90 JPEG of 1.5 MB, best of 5): stb_image 70.5 ms, libjpeg-turbo (turbojpeg, `TJFLAG_FASTDCT`, RGBA) 27.6 ms, 2.55 times faster, but the two IDCTs round differently, so baked pixels and goldens would change with the machine's libraries; 70 ms for a 12 MP photo does not justify a second decoder. If a program ever decodes many large photos at run time, libturbojpeg can be offered through the same plugin route as the camera plugin. |
| D35 | The style tasks no longer wait for the side records (ZN-251) | **The visible style features (sizes, flex, z-index, borders, typography, themes, queries) depend on the tasks they really need, not on ZN-251 and ZN-189.** The side records and the SoA node storage are a memory optimisation of `UiNode` (design: docs/reports/ui-style-system.md 5.3); until they land, a new property is one more field of `UiNode`, and ZN-251 later moves the extended ones into the record in one mechanical change that the pixel and dump goldens verify. Waiting for them held 20 tasks for a refactor that changes no behaviour. Scored: keep the chain 3, drop the dependency 7 (value to the owner now, same end state). |
| D36 | Spike: baseline JIT for ZBC with sljit (ZN-150) | **No-go, measured by the upper bound.** AOT is what a perfect JIT would reach, so its speed over the interpreter caps the JIT's (median of 11 runs, `bench/m4.json`, macOS arm64): fib 44.7 ms interpreter / 12.1 AOT (3.7x), nbody 494 / 110 (4.5x), mandelbrot 104 / 29 (3.6x), spectralnorm 775 / 65 (11.9x), sort 140 / 105 (1.3x), binarytrees 36 / 19 (1.9x), strings 47 / 34 (1.4x); a repainted UI page of 12 rows, buttons and a text field (`tests/golden/ui-alloc`): 0.09 ms per frame interpreted, 0.033 ms AOT (2.7x), and hero's paint phase is 0.26 ms (p50) in the interpreter. A baseline JIT without register allocation reaches about a third to a half of AOT on numeric code (1.5x to 3x here) and almost nothing on code that is bound by the runtime (strings, sort, allocation, host calls), which is what UI code is: it does not reach the criterion of 2x on UI and numeric code. Every hosted target already has AOT (`zinc build`); the interpreter is 5x QuickJS. A JIT adds executable-memory handling (W^X, entitlements on macOS, impossible on iOS and consoles), a second code path to keep bit-identical with the interpreter and AOT, and a 16 KB+ dependency. Revisit only if an app must load new code at run time on a device without a compiler and the interpreter proves too slow there. JavaScript libraries on QuickJS are a separate question: D44. |
| D37 | Where plugins live, and the GitHub organization (owner, 2026-10-08; ZN-335, ZN-352) | **An organization `zinc-engine` holds the engine, one repository per official plugin, the signed index, a plugin starter and the app templates. Official plugins stay developed in the monorepo (`plugins/`) and are published as mirror repositories (`git subtree split`, tags, their own CI) while the native ABI moves (`ZN_ABI_VERSION` 1); a plugin leaves the monorepo for good only once the ABI is frozen (ZN-353). Community plugins live in their authors' repositories: the index points at any git source and trust comes from signatures and rebuilds (ZN-340, ZN-341), not from the host.** Scores (fit x3, performance x3, size x2, maintainability x2, licence/supply x2, portability x1, effort x1): mirrors first 5,3,3,5,4,3,4 = 55; separate repositories now 4,3,3,2,4,3,2 = 44 (one PR per plugin on every ABI change); monorepo only 2,3,3,4,3,3,5 = 43 (no per-plugin versions, CI or outside maintainers). Creating the organization and its repositories is the owner's step. | Revisit when the ABI is frozen, or when a plugin gets an outside maintainer. |
| D38 | Where the Nuxt UI-style kit lives (ZN-357, owner: "make the best choice", 2026-10-08) | **A std module, `zinc:ui/nuxt` in `lib/std/nuxt/`**, written once for both UI models through the kit's `@jsxHelpers ./host` like `zinc:ui/kit`; `examples/nuxt-ui` is its gallery and dashboard app. Scores (fit x3, perf x3, size x2, maintainability x2, licence x2, portability x1, effort x1): std module 15+9+6+6+10+4+3 = **53**; example-local kit 9+9+6+8+10+4+4 = 50. The std module wins on fit: the app templates of M19 (ZN-315..) and any app can import it, as they import `zinc:ui/kit`; the cost is a public API to keep stable and the module registration. Nuxt UI's theme is Tailwind classes, which `zinc:ui` already reads, so the kit maps its classes rather than porting Vue code; its tokens carry the MIT notice of nuxt/ui. Revisit if the kit stays used by the example only after M19's templates land. |
| D39 | How AppImages are made (ZN-320.03) | **appimagetool (MIT, pinned with its SHA-256 like zig) on a Linux host or the Linux CI job; `zinc export --appimage` on macOS refuses and says so.** An AppImage is the type-2 runtime ELF followed by a squashfs image, and nothing on this Mac writes or reads squashfs (no mksquashfs, unsquashfs, appimagetool; the runtime's squashfuse is Linux-only), so no option can be verified here, and the AC itself is a run in a Linux container. Scores (fit x3, effort x2, licence x2, reach x2, risk x2): appimagetool on Linux 12+10+10+4+10 = **46**; our own uncompressed squashfs writer plus the pinned type-2 runtime 15+4+10+10+4 = 43 (any host, but a file-system writer is the kind of format the rules say not to reinvent, and it is unverifiable without Linux); libsquashfs of squashfs-tools-ng built with zig 15+4+4+10+6 = 39 (LGPL-3 inside the engine). Parked with the Linux-only runs (ZN-054 family, ZN-393): the .deb (ZN-320.01) already covers Debian-family Linux from any host. | Revisit when a Linux runner exists in the CI (then the pinned appimagetool), or if macOS-to-Linux AppImages are asked for (then the own writer, tested on that runner). |
| D40 | What zinc carries (ZN-330, owner 2026-10-08: "everything optional, the engine as agnostic as possible") | **Four verdicts, measured on macOS arm64** (bytes written into zinc from a linker map, zero-fill excluded, total 9.4 MB of code and data in an 11.7 MB file; clean build time of each library alone at -j3, 106 s for everything). **Plugin module, done:** WebGL (src/gl, glad, glslang) in `libzn_webgl` loaded on the first QuickJS context (ZN-330.01): zinc 13.98 to 11.66 MB; `-DZN_WEBGL=OFF` skips its 16 s of build. **Plugin module, next:** SDL3 1.56 MB / 10 s as the window backend (ZN-330.03); HarfBuzz + SheenBidi 1.09 MB / 15 s for `"text": "shaped"` only (ZN-330.04). **Optional build flag (exists):** mbedTLS 0.57 MB / 5 s (`ZN_TLS`: https, MQTT over TLS, crypto.subtle); the C test module `Fixture` 3 KB (`ZN_NATIVE_FIXTURE`); the graphics host (`ZN_HOST_GFX`). **Core, kept:** the compiler (frontend 1.51 MB / 11 s, ir 0.36 MB / 4 s, zbc 0.15 MB, aot 0.08 MB), the runtime and interpreter (rt 0.11 MB, vm 0.07 MB, mimalloc 0.10 MB, regexp 0.09 MB: RegExp of every program), zinc:gfx and the headless HAL (0.27 MB / 4 s), the resource baker (0.25 MB / 8 s: `zinc build` bakes fonts and images), the toolchain manager (0.26 MB), yyjson (0.21 MB), libunibreak (0.14 MB: Intl.Segmenter and line breaking), Yoga (0.07 MB / 4 s: the rn layout of every zinc:ui program that asks for it), libuv + llhttp (0.14 MB: zinc:http and the event loop), libyaml (0.06 MB), libwebp (0.37 MB / 3 s: WebP assets and `zinc capture`), QuickJS-ng (0.77 MB / 6 s: the second engine of D3 and zinc:script of D22; it becomes a module only with the minimal per-architecture zinc of ZN-348), and the CLI tools (sim, prof, lsp, dev, templates: 0.4 MB together). Scores per part (size saved x3, users who need it x3 inverted, effort x2, risk x2, 1-5): WebGL 5,5,4,4 = 46; SDL3 5,3,2,2 = 32; shaped text 4,4,3,4 = 38; mbedTLS as a module 3,2,3,4 = 29 (most network programs want https: the flag is enough); QuickJS 4,2,2,3 = 28; Yoga, libuv, libyaml, libwebp, unibreak 1-2,1-2,3,4 = 20 to 26 (under 0.4 MB each: a module costs more in loading code and packaging than it saves). | Revisit with ZN-348 (a runtime-only zinc per architecture drops the compiler, the CLI tools and QuickJS), or if a part grows past 0.5 MB. **ZPK reader and decoders (ZN-433, macOS x86_64):** 215.6 KB as zinc links them (reader 7.0, lz4 70.3, zstd decoder 136.6), 127.0 KB dead-stripped; core (lz4 everywhere, zstd off for ESP32); the writer's 0.57 MB only in the CLI. |
| D41 | The TUF client of the plugin and template index (ZN-336.01) | **A minimal C++ client of the TUF 1.0 specification in `src/tc` (root chain, timestamp, snapshot, targets, delegations by path, thresholds, expiry, rollback and hash checks), over the Ed25519 of monocypher already vendored (D-update, ZN-324), yyjson and OLPC canonical JSON; metadata in the spec's own format, so the repository side can be written by python-tuf (reference implementation, MIT / Apache-2.0) and an interop test reads python-tuf's metadata with our client.** Evidence: no maintained general-purpose C or C++ TUF client exists (the TUF project and Repository Service for TUF list python-tuf, go-tuf, tough in Rust and tuf-js; Uptane's aktualizr is an automotive stack around Boost, libsodium and OSTree); the client side of the spec is about 600 lines of verification logic over primitives we already ship; python-tuf 6.0.0 installs with pip for the CI and the interop test. Scores (fit x3, performance x3, size x2, maintainability x2, licence x2, portability x1, effort x1, 1-5): minimal C++ client 5,5,5,3,5,5,3 = **64**; tough as a Rust static library 3,5,3,4,5,3,2 = 53 (a Rust toolchain in every build of zinc and of the cross targets); go-tuf as a helper binary 2,4,2,4,5,3,3 = 46 (8 to 10 MB per target, a process per check); python-tuf's ngclient 2,3,1,5,5,2,4 = 43 (a Python runtime on every machine, the ESP32 path impossible); aktualizr 2,4,1,2,3,2,1 = 33. The attacks the client must refuse are the spec's (freeze, rollback, mix-and-match, arbitrary package, threshold, delegation) and are its tests (ZN-336.02). | Revisit if a maintained C or C++ TUF client appears, or if TAP 4 (multiple repositories) or TAP 8 (key rotation) is needed. |
| D42 | The transparency log of publications (ZN-343.01) | **A static Merkle log beside the index on Pages: RFC 6962 / 9162 hashing (leaf `SHA-256(0x00 || entry)`, node `SHA-256(0x01 || left || right)`), one entry per published artifact (its index path and SHA-256), a checkpoint (size, root) signed with Ed25519 in the index's canonical JSON form, an inclusion proof per artifact and a consistency proof from every earlier published size, all written by `tools/tlog` in the publishing job; the client (`src/tc/tlog.cpp`) checks the checkpoint signature, the inclusion of what it installs and the consistency with the last checkpoint it saw.** The construction is the one of the Go checksum database and Sigsum (static files, signed checkpoints, proofs the client checks itself); Sigstore Rekor is the same idea as a service. Scores (fit x3, performance x3, size x2, maintainability x2, licence x2, portability x1, effort x1, 1-5): static log on Pages 5,5,5,4,5,5,4 = **67** (same host, mirrors and offline cache as the index, no service to run, about 150 lines over the SHA-256 and Ed25519 zinc ships); Sigstore's public Rekor 3,3,3,3,5,3,2 = 45 (an external service and its API versions, a network round trip per check, no C++ client, its own trust root); a self-hosted Rekor 3,4,3,2,5,3,1 = 45 (a server and its database to operate). A malicious but signed release then has to be appended where everyone sees it, and a log that forks or rewrites its history fails the consistency check of every client that saw it before. | Revisit when witnesses can cosign checkpoints (C2SP tlog-witness), or if the index moves to Sigstore. |
| D43 | What `zinc run` executes (ZN-398, owner 2026-10-09: "in the prototype, run was not the VM; even run had very good performance") | **A program that draws is compiled to native code and the executable is cached; the interpreter stays for `zinc dev`, `--interp` and test runs.** Measured on bouncing-ball with 200k balls in a window (macOS arm64, 10 s runs alternated): interpreted run 23.9 fps, compiled run 31.7, prototype run (native) 33.7; startup of a cached run 0.27 s against 0.23 s interpreted, first run of a version about 4 s more (the C++ build). Options scored (speed x3, first-start latency x2, dev loop x2, test determinism x2, effort x1; 1-5): (a) compile and cache for programs that draw, interpreter for dev, `--interp` and headless or deterministic runs: 5,3,5,5,4 = 42; (b) interpreter by default, `--release` flag: 2,5,5,5,5 = 37 (the owner's first impression is the slow path); (c) start interpreted and swap to the compiled program on the next run: 4,5,4,4,2 = 35 (two engines per session, a first run that misleads); (d) compile everything, command-line programs too: 5,2,3,3,4 = 33 (a script pays 4 s for no visible gain). The cache key is the program's C++ with its baked resources, zinc.json, the engine binary and libraries, the plugins and CXX: a change anywhere rebuilds; no compiler or a failed build falls back to the interpreter with a line on stderr. Command-line arguments now reach compiled programs (zinc:sys args()). ZN-605: the run cache also records the files the compile and the bake read (sources, zinc.json, assets, fonts, plugin directories, with times and sizes) and the engine and environment; a cached run checks them and starts the executable without compiling (bouncing-ball 0.27 s to 0.16 s, the app alone 0.15 s; hero about 1.9 s to 0.2 s, its compile being 1.2 s), and a changed input is compiled once in-process (no `zinc build` subprocess), the C++ key deciding whether the native build runs. | Revisit when the interpreter reaches compiled speed on drawing programs, or if the first-run build time grows past 10 s (then build in the background and run interpreted meanwhile). |
| D44 | QuickJS speed: AOT, JIT and the native boundary (QJS-01; `docs/reports/quickjs-aot-jit-and-ffi.md`) | **No QuickJS JIT. Fix the boundary and accelerate library hot spots now; QuickJS bytecode -> C AOT only through a gated spike.** Measured on the M1 Pro: QuickJS spends 2.9-4.8 ns per bytecode, 85-97% of samples in the interpreter loop, and per-opcode fast paths change workloads by 0-5%; V8 jitless is 1.7-2.7x faster (the ceiling of interpreter work), V8's JIT 8-36x. A hand-made bytecode -> C translation of the sprite loop is 5.9x faster (7.4x with inline caches), of three.js's `Matrix4.multiplyMatrices` 4.9x, bit-identical with `-ffp-contract=off`; speculative code 85x. The boundary costs 20-45 ns per call except typed arrays (1,360 ns: an exception used as a type test, ZN-567) and the host thunk (139-156 ns: two vectors); a typed fast-call path saves another 25-30%; JS-side command buffers cost 300-430 ns per command and are rejected; one native accelerator cuts the three.js frame by 24%. Options scored (gain x3, targets incl. iOS/consoles x2, effort x2, risk and maintenance x2, upstreamability x1; 1-5): (a) boundary fixes, typed fast calls, accelerators, bytecode cache: 2,5,5,5,4 = 40; (f) a JIT engine (JSC/V8) on desktop, ZN-586: 5,1,3,3,3 = 32; (b) bytecode -> C AOT with ICs: 3,5,2,3,2 = 31; (e) interpreter work in a fork (ICs, register VM, 64-bit NaN-boxing; upstream removed its ICs in 2025): 2,5,2,3,3 = 29; (d) optimizing JIT for QuickJS: 5,2,1,1,1 = 24; (g) Zinc's typed AOT for npm libraries: 1,5,1,2,3 = 22; (c) copy-and-patch baseline JIT: 3,2,1,2,1 = 20. Do (a) now (ZN-567, QJS-02/03/04/05/08, ZN-575), (b) as the spike QJS-06 (the only option that raises the ceiling on every target, iOS and consoles included) with a gate (>= 2x on the three.js and matter-js frames, code <= 15x the bytecode, test262 identical), (e) only as upstream proposals (QJS-07 if (b) is a no-go), (f) stays ZN-586; reject (c), (d), (g). Engine changes live in a patch queue on pristine quickjs-ng, each proposed upstream. | The interpreter, not the binding, is the compatibility tier's ceiling; native code is the only large lever, and only AOT native code runs on every Zinc target (iOS 9 and consoles forbid executable memory); Zinc already ships the C toolchain and a compile-and-cache run model (D43). Consistent with D13/D36: JS libraries cannot use the typed AOT, but their bytecode can be compiled to C. | QJS-06 below 1.5x on library frames (then QJS-07 and the native tier carry the load); a product that must run downloaded JavaScript fast on desktop (then ZN-586); quickjs-ng merging inline caches or a fast-call API upstream (then drop the patch). |
| D45 | Which fonts a program bakes (ZN-428) | **The embedded TrueType files are subset with hb-subset to the scanned characters plus Latin-1, Latin Extended-A and common punctuation (a `"text": "shaped"` project keeps the whole files); the grid font's 32, 48 and 64 px tables are baked only when the program's own files call `gfx.text`; the library's files count for sizes and characters (a kit Button really is `text-sm`) but not for faces and case maps (`italic`, `font-mono`, `uppercase` in `lib/std/ui.ts` are table entries, not uses); `gfx.font` keeps the prototype's answer, the closest baked size and -1 for a family not baked (an exact size rasterized from the embedded file changed tests/conformance/canvas2d, whose monospace falls back to sans in the prototype; reverted in ZN-604).** Measured with `zinc bake`: game-2d 1,706 KB to 307 KB, kit-gallery and text 3,576 KB to 786 KB, hero 6,805 KB (7.8 MB before the subset) to 1,467 KB; bake time unchanged (1.2-1.5 s for hero). Rejected: skipping the library's files altogether (kit-gallery 209 KB) because the runtime rasterizer (`runtime/ttf.cpp`, exact coverage) and the baker (4x4 supersampling) differ by up to 40 levels on antialiased edges, so baked sizes must cover what the program draws to keep the prototype's pixels (canary 1 of 4 against 4 of 4). | Revisit if the runtime rasterizer is made identical to the baker's (then only the program's own sizes need baking), or for MSDF fonts (ZN-442). |
| D46 | The AOT program diet, part 2 (ZN-604) | **A program with more than 2 MB of generated C++ is compiled as up to 3 translation units at once (contiguous functions with external linkage; the first unit has the tables and main; small programs such as the benchmarks stay one unit with static functions); the generated C++ is compiled without the stack protector (it indexes its frames with constants only) and linked without local symbols (`ZN_KEEP_SYMBOLS=1` keeps them for profilers); compiled reference counting skips the `zinc mem` statistics, which only the interpreter keeps (`op::retainC`, `op::releaseC`); exception handlers test their class out of line; the vendored SDL3 is built without its dynamic API table (a two-line patch behind `ZN_SDL_NO_DYNAPI`), whose pointers kept every SDL function from dead-stripping; the module and the baked resources are string literals instead of lists of integers; the checks the functions repeat are short macros.** Measured on hero (macOS arm64, M1 Pro, -j3): binary 9.65 to 7.62 MB, `zinc build` 43.8 to 13.9 s, generated C++ without the resource literal about 16 to 9.55 MB (13.7 MB with it); interpreter and AOT frame hashes identical on hero, nuxt-ui, rn-showcase and bouncing-ball. Rejected, measured: `retain` and `release` out of line (another 0.6 MB, but binarytrees +9.6%, mapset +4.4%, dynsum +2.5%); `releaseC` forced inline everywhere (+0.8 MB, +13 s, no kernel faster); `-Os` (7% less code, same time); `.incbin` of the resources (inline assembly that the cross and wasm builds do not all accept). | Revisit with ZN-427 (an object cache per function makes a one-function rebuild take seconds) and ZN-413 (fewer retains and releases to emit). |
| D47 | Tiles in the software raster (ZN-410) | **A frame of 2048 commands or more is binned once into 16 x 16 tiles, walking the commands from the last to the first: a tile takes a command unless an opaque square-cornered RECT or the CLEAR above it already covers it whole outside any clip, and the walk stops once every tile is covered; each band then paints its tiles' lists with the same code as `render()`, CLIP and UNCLIP replayed in every tile their box touches. A frame keeps `render()` when the tiles would repeat more set-up work than they hide (4 x the extra calls of rounded shapes, text, polygons and images against the hidden commands; then no binning for 30 frames), past 16 nested clips, or with `ZINC_TILES=0`.** Measured (`zinc capture --bench`, M1 Pro): the 200k-ball scene at 1280x960 22.4 to 8.3 ms on 1 thread, 13.9 to 6.3 ms on 4, same pixel hash; the render corpus keeps its hashes, b1-quads 1.77 to 1.23 ms on 1 thread, the others within noise. Rejected: two forward passes (count, then fill) 12.9 ms, the binning being 77% of it; binning every large frame (b2-rounded, 6000 overlapping rounded rects, 14 to 54 ms: each tile sets up the shape's rows again). | Revisit with a GPU backend by default (PERF-13), or to bin in parallel when one band thread binning stalls the others. |
| D48 | How a WebGL canvas reaches the page (ZN-411) | **`gl.zincPresent` scales the canvas down and flips it on the GPU (`glBlitFramebuffer`, linear for the 2x supersampling: the average of 4 texels) and reads it into a pixel buffer object without waiting; the read is copied into the Surface's image in the next frame's present, or at the host's frame end when no present follows (a canvas drawn on demand). The canvas is shown one frame late, like a browser compositor; deterministic runs read in the same frame (`ZINC_GL_PRESENT=sync|async` chooses). GLES 2 and 3x / 4x supersampling keep the full read averaged on the CPU.** Measured on webgl-cube in a window (Retina, 1440x960 canvas, `sample` of the main thread): `zincPresent` 79% to 2.8% (the full read: 30%, of which the GPU wait 32% and the driver's detiling 21% once the downsampling moved to the GPU; the CPU 2x2 average 45%); 65 to 80 fps with frame hashing on. Rejected: a synchronous read after the GPU downsampling (the wait for the GPU stays, 62% of the main thread); composing the canvas texture on the GPU (the window HAL composes on the CPU; with a GPU 2D backend, PERF-13, the canvas texture can be drawn without any read); shadow state for `compositeClear` (not visible in the samples). | Revisit with the GPU 2D backend (PERF-13), or if a program needs the canvas in the same frame outside tests. |
| D49 | Fewer GPU primitives in the GL replay (ZN-412.01) | **The display-gl renderer draws the opaque square RECTs and CLEARs outside clips first, front to back with depth writes, through a program of their own (one 24-byte instance a rectangle on desktop GL, four 16-byte vertices on GLES 2), then every other command depth-tested at its place in the paint order; a list of 2048 commands or more is first culled on the CPU (`zrt::raster::cull`: 8 px tiles, walking back from the last command, rows of open tiles as 64-bit masks, superblocks of 8 x 8 tiles), so commands covered whole are never submitted. The frames are the same with and without either (`ZINC_GL_OCCLUDE`, `ZINC_GL_CULL`).** Measured on bouncing-ball 200k in a window (M1 Pro, 640x480 surface, GPU timer queries): replay 15.4 ms (GPU done) and 5.5 ms of CPU before; occlusion alone 4.0 ms of GPU and 2.0 ms of CPU (a tiling GPU removes hidden pixels itself, the primitives are what cost); with culling 1.4 ms of GPU (46k of 200k quads) and 3.6 ms of CPU, 2.9 of it culling. Rejected: culling on 4 threads by bands of tile rows (slower: every band reads every command); 16 px tiles for culling (balls of 8 to 18 px rarely cover one). ZN-412.05: the display-gl surface of bouncing-ball is 320x240, so the tiles now take 2/3 of the opaque rectangles' mean size (4 px there: 7.9k quads instead of 46k), the culling list comes out of the walk front to back with the opaque ones flagged (no pass over every command afterwards), and each command's tile range is packed first on 4 threads (the memory-bound half), the walk reading 8 bytes a command: 0.66 ms of GPU and 1.77 ms of CPU submission. A software prefetch in the walk gained nothing. | Revisit with ZN-590 (GPU strokes and fills). |
| D50 | Which loads lend their value without a retain (ZN-413) | **Each load (GetField, GetGlobal, ArrGet, Map.get) is judged on its own: its value is lent, with no retain and no release, when no use consumes it (a call argument, a store, a return, an edge) and no instruction in its live range may release a reference or run program code: calls, stores of references, console output, a ToStr of an object, and the runtime rows that release or call back (`rtMayRelease`, false for the string rows, the read-only Array, Map and Set members and the graphics host). What it was read from stays alive meanwhile: a global (nothing in the range can store to it) or the anchor, the tracked value at the root of the chain of loads, whose last use moves to the lent value's last use. This replaces the rule that lent only in functions without any call. `ZN_NO_LEND=1` retains every load, to measure.** Measured: bouncing-ball's frame function (everything inlined) keeps 1 retain (the HUD's) instead of 9; `zinc mem` at 200k balls, 1 retain per frame instead of 400k; the compiled 200k-ball loop 2.70-2.85 to 2.51-2.68 ms per frame (headless, 1100 minus 100 frames, two alternations). An ASan and UBSan build ran 107 programs (the run goldens, the kernels, `tests/data/rc_lend.ts`) without a report and with the same `ZN_LEAK_CHECK` results with and without lending; run goldens and destruction order unchanged; 8 ZBC disassembly goldens regenerated (fewer RC ops, shifted offsets). Not done: borrowed parameters (a callee that never consumes a parameter does not release it and its callers do not retain it; a convention per function and per vtable slot, ZN-606); the release fast path inline was measured in D46 (+0.8 MB, no kernel faster) and stays out of line; compiled code already skips the `zinc mem` counters (D46). | Revisit when a profile shows retain and release pairs around calls of methods that are not inlined (ZN-606), or if a runtime row is added that releases or calls back without being listed. |
| D51 | Borrowed parameters (ZN-417, ZN-606) | **A reference parameter that its function never consumes (no store, no return, no edge, no call argument whose callee owns it) is borrowed: the caller keeps its reference across the call and the callee neither retains nor releases it. The convention is inferred over the whole program as a greatest fixed point, per function and per vtable slot (a slot borrows what all its methods borrow, and its methods follow the slot); the methods the runtime calls (`call` of closures, `__errorString`) keep owned parameters, because `runClosure`, `callComparator` and `exceptionText` hand over a reference. The native module ABI is unchanged (native calls never consumed). With it, a call of a function that cannot store a reference (no store of a reference, no runtime row that releases or calls back, no console output, no native call, no throw, and only calls of such functions; per vtable slot for virtual calls) no longer ends the live range of a lent load (D50). `ZN_NO_BORROW=1` restores the previous convention, to measure.** Chosen over ZN-417's first plan (every parameter borrowed by default, the callee retaining what it keeps, a native ABI version bump): inference changes no ABI and no back end, since retains and releases are IR operations every engine runs as written. Measured on the Intel Mac: hero start (zinc mem, 1 frame) 70,130 to 56,398 retains (-20%), 60 frames 324,597 to 240,119 (-26%), allocations and frees unchanged; AOT script time of maps/navigation's paint 7.18 to 7.05 ms per frame (-2%), hero's frames unchanged (a retain is an inline increment in compiled code). ASan and UBSan over 132 programs (run goldens, the M3 conformance set, the kernels, examples/lang): no report; `rc` (no leak over the corpus, the frozen destruction order) passes; 10 ZBC disassembly goldens regenerated. The rest of hero's start is stores of shared sentinel objects into the fields of each UiNode (13,770 retains in its constructor), which a store must count: ZN-616. | Revisit if the runtime starts calling another method by name (it must then keep owned parameters), or with ZN-616's immortal constants. |
| D52 | Immortal constant objects (ZN-616) | **Not done: shared defaults read from module constants and stored into fields keep their retain and release.** Measured on the Intel Mac, AOT: 2,000,000 objects each storing 6 shared sentinels into fields (12 M retains, and 12 M releases when they die) take 125-132 ms, the same loop storing null 89-91 ms: about 1.5 ns per retain or release. hero's start does 13,770 such retains in UiNode.constructor, about 40 us of a 200 ms start (0.4 ms on a Pi 3, ten times slower). Making the objects of never-reassigned constants immortal would remove that, but the program-end teardown would then have to free them after everything else and release their children without freeing twice when two constants point at each other, and the frozen destruction order (tests/golden/rc/order.trace) would change: real risk (a use-after-free at exit) for no visible gain. Retain and release counts are not a cost target by themselves: D50 and D51 were kept because they also removed work (2-26% fewer operations in loops, -2% paint on navigation). | Revisit if a profile of a real program shows retain or release above 2% of its frame or start time. |
| D53 | Interpreter hot paths (ZN-421) | **Not done: the three changes of PERF-22 do not pay on the measured loop, so the interpreter is unchanged.** bouncing-ball with 200k balls, interpreted, headless, `ZINC_PROFILE` effects p50 over 120 frames, idle Intel Mac, 3 alternations: the original 18.98-19.11 ms (AOT 8.2 ms). Testing the hostFast table in `L_Rt` before `rtCall` (which tests it first anyway): 19.05-19.28, no gain. A `SetField` that skips the field-kind look-up (upper bound, unsafe for reference fields): 17.31-17.46 (-9%). Done safely (the verifier marks the stores whose field holds no reference, the interpreter rewrites them in its code copy into an internal opcode, no ZBC change): 20.5-20.9, slower, and 20.5 with the rewrite switched off in the same binary: adding a handler to the dispatch function changes the compiler's code for the whole loop by more than the store saves. Globals are already one indexed load. The loop runs about 45 instructions per ball at about 2 ns each: what would pay is fewer instructions (constant globals as immediates, fused field updates such as `x += vx * dt`, the `width()`/`height()` host calls per ball), measured with the M4 kernels to keep the dispatch layout honest. | Revisit with a plan that cuts the instruction count, or when the interpreter runs on a device core where it is the only engine. |
| D54 | Profile-guided builds of zinc (ZN-424) | **Not adopted: the release build stays without a profile.** `tools/pgo` builds an instrumented zinc (`-fprofile-instr-generate`), runs the training set (the M4 kernels interpreted, a compile of examples/hero, 90 headless frames of hero, nuxt-ui and bouncing-ball), merges the 10.7 MB profile with llvm-profdata and builds zinc with `-fprofile-instr-use` (Apple clang 17, -O2). Measured against the normal build on the idle Intel Mac (median of 5): M4 kernels interpreted from -6% (dynsum) to +7% (fib, empty), about -1% overall; a compile of hero -2%; bouncing-ball 200k interpreted +2%; hero's frames unchanged. The task kept PGO only at 5% or more. The binary is smaller (11.9 against 13.8 MB) since cold code is optimised for size. | Revisit with another compiler or flags (-O3, LTO across runtime and host, PERF-24), on another machine (a Pi, where caches are small), or with a training set from a real application: `tools/pgo` does the whole measurement. |
| D55 | QuickJS bytecode to C, the qjs2c spike (ZN-599, QJS-06 of D44) | **No-go: no ahead-of-time compilation of QuickJS bytecode; the levers of D44 (native accelerators, inline caches as an upstream proposal, ZN-600) carry the speed of JavaScript libraries.** `tools/qjs2c` copies the interpreter's own opcode bodies per instruction (constant operands, direct jumps, no dispatch; exceptions return to the interpreter's frame; patch 0002-aot-hooks binds at the first call). Measured on the idle Intel Mac against the interpreter, the 30 hottest functions of each workload compiled: three.js 500 meshes 7.31 -> 5.71 ms (1.28x), sprite 1.40x, objects 1.08x, matter-js 300 bodies 9.42 -> 7.32 ms (1.29x); the gate was 2x on three.js and matter-js. Code is 53-61x the bytecode (gate 15x). Correctness holds: the QuickJS T1 programs (483 functions compiled) and the test262 regexp subset (861 functions, 2,533 of 2,557, the same failures) match the interpreter. 71-72% of the time stays inside the compiled bodies: stack traffic through memory (a C-array stack measured the same: it escapes into the slow paths), a refcount pair per local read, a shape lookup per property access, integer-only fast paths, the generic call entry. Report: `docs/reports/zinc-next-qjs2c.md`. | A copy of the interpreter removes only the dispatch, as quickjs-aot (+36%) and the quickjs-ng maintainer (1.2-1.5x) said; the hand translations' 5-7x come from compiler work (stack to SSA, borrowing, inline caches, unboxed doubles), i.e. an optimizing JIT done ahead of time, a project of its own with a 15x+ code cost. | A product needs 2x or more on JavaScript library frames on a target without a JIT (iOS, consoles) after accelerators and inline caches; or quickjs-ng gains inline caches or a register VM upstream (smaller, faster bodies to copy: rerun the gate with `tools/qjs2c`, kept off by default); or a partial evaluator of C interpreters (weval-style) becomes usable with clang. |
| D56 | Inline caches in the QuickJS interpreter (ZN-600, QJS-07 of D44 after D55) | **Not adopted: the QuickJS interpreter keeps its own property lookup.** Monomorphic per-site caches for get_field, get_field2, put_field and get_var (own properties checked by the atom at the cached index; prototype properties up to three deep through referenced hashed shapes; globals guarded by global_var_obj's shape and size; a table per function after 32 calls or back jumps) hit 89-99.9% of accesses and change nothing that matters on the idle Intel Mac: three.js 0.98-1.00x, sprite 0.97-0.98x, objects 1.01-1.06x, matter-js 0.98-1.02x (gate: 15% on two workloads, none slower); the classic variant that hits on one shape comparison is slower (sprite 0.94-0.95x). Correct on test262 regexp and the QuickJS T1 programs; 1.6-5 KB per warmed function. The patch is kept as `docs/reports/zinc-next-qjs-ic.patch`, report `docs/reports/zinc-next-qjs-ic.md`. | QuickJS's inlined `find_own_property` already costs about what a cache check costs, the interpreter's time is in dispatch, stack and refcounts (D55), and the larger `JS_CallInternal` loses a few percent; upstream removed its caches in 2025 (1.037x) and closed a monomorphic one in 2026 for flat results. | ZN-600.01 measures the patch on Apple Silicon (the criterion named the M1 Pro) and adopts it as patch 0003 only if it passes the gate there; or quickjs-ng changes its property lookup or its dispatch. |
| D57 | The typed WebGL API of compiled Zinc code, zinc:gl (ZN-562) | **zinc:gl keeps WebGL's names and argument order and types what WebGL leaves as `any`; the QuickJS binding is not moved onto zinc:gl's rows.** Every method of the QuickJS binding (243, WebGL 1 and 2 and the extensions' methods) has a zinc:gl method of the same name, generated with its host row and C++ from one table (`tools/gen-zinc-gl`). Typed arrays are `GLArray` subclasses holding a Zinc array and an element kind (readPixels and getBufferSubData fill the array given); the queries that return `any` return a `GLParam` (kind, numbers, text, object id); `getExtension` returns a boolean and the extension's methods are the context's (`createVertexArrayOES`...); `bufferData` with a size is `bufferDataSize`; `present(image)` replaces `zincPresent`; a lost context is simplified (no DOM events; `restoreContext` gives a new context). Both bindings call the same `WebGL1` class, which holds the validation; `tests/golden/webgl/api_tour.{js,ts}` prints the same 27 lines through QuickJS and zinc:gl (interpreted and built). | Moving the QuickJS binding onto the rows would add the HostCall decoding (about 40 ns, ZN-562.03) to every JS call and change no validation; a typed API cannot return `any`, and generating the 245 rows keeps the three layers in step. | The WebGL1 conformance runs through zinc:gl (a port of the pages to Zinc), or a type of `any` lands in Zinc. |
| D58 | zinc:3d on the GPU (ZN-563) | **A second native backend of zinc:3d, render3d.gl.cpp (GL 3.2 core / GLES 3 subset, its own SDL3 context, like display-gl), reproduces the software renderer's shading; option `renderer`: `auto` (default) = the GPU where the target has the backend (macOS now) unless the run is deterministic, `software`, `gpu`; ZINC_3D overrides it.** Same interface as the software renderer, which it includes as the fallback (no context) and the oracle: per-vertex Lambert lighting clamped per channel, flat faces from screen derivatives, nearest texels, colour in screen space, triangles submitted counted (three.js's semantics). The frame is read into the runtime image one frame late through a pixel buffer (synchronous in deterministic runs), RGBA bytes with the shaders writing blue first; the colour attachment is a texture because Apple's GL fills a pixel buffer read from an RGBA8 renderbuffer with garbage. The GL calls are direct, not zinc:gl's rows (C++ to C++, no validation per call). The window's GL present now makes its own context current and puts the caller's back (the host swapped zinc:3d's hidden context, without vsync) | Scored: GPU backend in C++ 4/5 (2.4 ms for 1000 meshes vs 3.2 software and 73 for three.js on QuickJS, one source for GL and GLES), the same renderer in Zinc over zinc:gl 2/5 (4+ host calls per mesh, a decoded array per matrix), Phong / Standard per pixel now 1/5 (no specular or roughness in the interface; frames would leave the oracle) | A scene where the software renderer wins at default size (small scenes on a fast CPU): `renderer: "software"` in zinc.json; Phong / Standard with an interface that carries them |
| D59 | iOS and Android targets (ZN-372, the mobile spike) | **Both are viable: go, Android first, in the cross-platform pass at the end. Hosting: an SDL3 shell, the static engine with `targets/macos/hal_sdl.cpp` on SDL3's UIKit and Android backends. Toolchain: `zinc export --target ios\|android` generates an Xcode or Gradle project that the platform clang builds, signs and packages; the pinned zig is not used for mobile. Target ids `ios`, `ios-sim`, `android`.** Scores (RULES §4, max 70), hosting: SDL3 shell 62, our own UIKit and Android HALs 53, a web view around the wasm build 42, `zinc:ui` on native widgets 33; toolchain: generated project 60, pinned zig with the platform sysroots 47, prebuilt engine with bytecode only 42. The shell reuses the one HAL and keeps pixels and demos unchanged; the generated project is what every reference product does and the only option that meets Apple's Xcode requirement without a second toolchain. Store builds: AOT by default, the interpreter and QuickJS only with code bundled in the package, plugins linked statically, no `dlopen`, no `zinc:process` on iOS, over-the-air `.zapp` updates off by default on iOS, `zinc dev` reload in debug builds only. Pixels: the software raster through `SDL_Renderer` first, GLES 3 replay on Android next, the iOS GPU back end a later measured decision. Nothing was built for a phone: the development Mac has neither Xcode nor the Android SDK, so the runs on a simulator and an emulator (criterion 1 of ZN-372) are tasks of M26. Revisit when SDL3's text input or lifecycle proves inadequate on a device (then a HAL of our own for that platform), when the software raster misses 60 Hz on a mid-range phone, or when zig ships bionic and an iOS SDK. Full record, sources and what is not verified: `docs/reports/zinc-next-mobile-spike.md` (sections 10 and 11). |
| D60 | The network log of the inspector (ZN-370) | **One recorder in the host (`src/host/netlog.cpp`), not in the plugins: fetch, MQTT and sockets all end in `src/host`, so it sees every request and the HAR export (`zinc run --har`, `ZINC_HAR`) works without the inspector.** One multiplexed row `host.netLog(op, arg)` (start, drain events, response body, request body) instead of four, because the host table is shared with the device cores and the zinc:gl rows. WebSockets are decoded from the socket bytes in the host (no hook in `plugins/socket`, shared with the prototype); MQTT clients and WebSockets both use the protocol's WebSocket events, other outgoing TCP is a `tcp://host:port` request; a redirect is one entry per hop, as in a HAR. Recording starts when `zinc:devtools` loads, so requests made before DevTools attaches are listed; the inspector's own loopback port is not recorded. Bounds: the last 1000 entries, 4 MiB per body, 10000 messages per stream, 8 MiB of undrained events. Secrets are masked at record time: Authorization, Proxy-Authorization, Cookie, Set-Cookie, header names containing token, secret, password, api-key, session or credential, the names in `ZINC_NET_SENSITIVE`, and `user:password@` in URLs; query parameters, form fields and JSON fields named in `ZINC_NET_SENSITIVE` are masked too, and the HAR file is kept current while the program runs (both D63). Checked by `tests/t0/netlog.sh` (7 requests in the Network domain, HAR 1.2, TLS timings); the DevTools panel itself was exercised through the protocol and Chrome's HAR importer, not looked at. |
| D61 | Strokes on the GPU: a STROKE command (ZN-590.01) | **A display that replays the command lists on the GPU (the macOS GL window, display-gl) sets `gfx::gpu_strokes`; `gfx.stroke()` then records a STROKE command (the polyline in physical pixels, its width, closed in `pad` bit 1) instead of `stroke_contours`' outline. The GL replay draws one box per segment and the fragment shader takes the capsule distance (round joins and caps), a pixel belonging to the nearest of the segment and its two neighbours so it is blended once; the software raster tessellates a STROKE it meets (same outline, same frames at scale 1).** Without a GPU display nothing changes (the outline is recorded, goldens untouched); `ZINC_GPU_STROKES=0|1` forces either way. Differences from the software frame: round caps on lines thinner than 2 px (the software has none), analytic edges instead of 4x4 supersampling, a self-crossing translucent stroke blends twice where non-adjacent segments overlap | Scored: STROKE + capsule shader 5/5 (navigation in the GL window 232-247 -> 12.8 ms per frame, 836 CPU tiles -> 0.1), tessellating on the CPU and filling on the GPU 2/5 (needs the POLY fills of ZN-590.02 first, the outline is 10-40x the points), keeping the CPU tiles 0/5 | Strokes drawn with miter joins or dashes; plugins (canvas2d, svg) that tessellate their own strokes moving to STROKE |
| D62 | POLY fills on the GPU: stencil then cover with distance fringes (ZN-590.02) | **The GL replay fills LINE/POLY with the stencil: the contours' triangle fans count the winding at the pixel centres (7 bits, nonzero by facing, even-odd by bit 0); the pixels near an edge get their coverage from the distance to it (an EDGE quad per edge, owned by the nearest of the edge and its neighbours), inside only where the edge bounds the fill there (winding 1 on its left, -1 on its right, so overlapping contours leave no seam), each pixel blended once through bit 7; a cover quad fills the rest; the stencil is zeroed over the box. The fringe quads are uploaded once and drawn by each pass.** The canvas gradient paint (grad 4) and a framebuffer without 8 stencil bits keep the CPU coverage tiles; `ZINC_GL_POLY=0` forces them. The framebuffer is depth 24 / stencil 8 (OES_packed_depth_stencil on GLES 2), the GL windows ask for 8 stencil bits. The svg plugin emits STROKE commands for its plain strokes when the display draws them (exactly abutting contours of a tessellated stroke would still show a seam) | Scored: stencil + distance fringes 4/5 (no CPU geometry for animated content: lottie 11.4 -> 2.8 ms, hero 4.0 -> 0.5, svg 3.6 -> 1.4, canvas 9.7 -> 3.9 to submit; GLES 2), CPU triangulation (libtess2 / earcut) cached by shape 2/5 (re-run every frame for moving content, a new vendored library), MSAA 1/5 (none on GLES 2, 4 samples against the raster's 16, changes every other command's edges), the CPU tiles 1/5 (836 per frame for a moving map) | Coincident opposite edges (abutting contours) in POLY input; a static scene where the tile cache was cheaper (navigation at rest: 1.0 -> 1.4 ms to submit) |
| D63 | The inspector port, masked fields and a HAR that survives a kill (ZN-633, ZN-635, ZN-636) | **The inspector port reaches the program as `ZINC_DEVTOOLS_PORT`, which `zinc run` sets from zinc.json `plugins.devtools.port` (0 takes a free port, named on stderr); fields of queries and bodies are masked for the names of `ZINC_NET_SENSITIVE` only; the HAR file is rewritten while the program runs, at most once a second, on the loop's own turns.** Port: an environment variable set by the CLI, like `ZINC_KEYBOARD` and `ZINC_SCHEME` (one consumer, and `main.cpp` stays wiring only); a board file's `plugins.devtools` is not read and `zinc build` does not bake the port. Masking: the built-in header words (token, session...) do not apply to bodies, where such fields are often the data being debugged; masked at record time are query parameters (request URLs, WebSocket upgrade targets, `Location`), urlencoded form fields and JSON fields at any depth (request and response bodies, WebSocket and MQTT text). JSON fields are found by a scanner of strings and brackets in `netlog.cpp` (about 80 lines), not by yyjson: the host library links no JSON parser (yyjson would enter every AOT link line), the body must stay byte for byte, and truncated or line-delimited JSON is masked too; it builds no values, so it is a tokenizer, not the parser that RULES §3 forbids. Not masked: free text, multipart forms (ZN-640), plain TCP bytes, names written percent-encoded or with JSON escapes, JSON inside a JSON string. HAR: `loop::pump()` and `loop::wait()` call `netlog::tick`, which writes the whole file and renames it over the target when the log changed; no signal handler (it cannot build the HAR safely) and no libuv timer (a repeating timer wakes `loop::wait` early, and due `setTimeout`s then fire early); the file may be a second behind at a kill. Checked by `tests/t0/devtools_port.sh` (two programs inspected at once), `netlog.sh` and `netlog_kill.sh` (SIGTERM and SIGKILL; the test fails on the binary before the change). The tests take free ports from `tests/free_ports.py`, so the socket, dev_mode and netlog tests of two trees no longer collide (UDP ports of socket.ts: ZN-639). Not measured: the cost of rewriting a large log; not proven: an AOT-compiled run. Revisit when the host library links a JSON parser, or when a board file needs its own port. |
| D64 | lib/std stays inside what the prototype compiles (ZN-637) | **`lib/std/ui.ts` and what it imports keep compiling with the prototype, and `tests/t1/proto_devtools.sh` (the prototype builds and runs a zinc:ui + zinc:devtools app in the sim and as a macOS build, checked by `scripts/cdp-check.mjs`, about 25 s) guards it for every change to `lib/std/ui.ts`.** Found by ZN-637: the new `net` member of the devtools Spec had broken nothing, but the prototype could build no zinc:ui program since 2026-10-08, so `tools/proto-capture capture` and `tools/example-output` could make no new prototype golden. Causes, each fixed where it broke: `commandCount` / `commandsFree` missing from `lib/gfx.d.ts` and the sim; `UI_LAYOUT`, `UI_PRESET` and the module `zinc:__layout` known to Zinc Next only (now constants of the prototype's `zinc:platform` and a stub `lib/compat/proto-layout.ts`); `onResize` missing from the prototype runtime and the sim; a `Map.get()` number compared with `undefined` in `propId` (`?? 0` now); a closure that calls itself in `inspect()` (a module function now; the prototype accepts such a closure and emits C++ that does not compile, ZN-642). Rejected: a frozen copy of `lib/std` for the prototype (two libraries to keep equal), and recursive closures in the prototype's emitter (the prototype is touched for fixes only). `onResize` in the prototype is accepted and never called: a port of ZN-608 would change the prototype's windows and needs teardown on hot reload; no task is filed for it. Checked on Zinc Next after the change: ui, ui_style, jsx_style, ui_a11y, perf_hud, scale_push pass and the build stays warning-free. Left: the prototype's sim conformance fails on three zinc:net programs for another reason (ZN-641). |
| D65 | When a program's console output is written (ZN-638) | **At each turn of the event loop, not per line and not only at exit.** The typed engines kept `console.log` in `Machine::out` until exit, on a terminal as well as into a file or a pipe (only QuickJS used stdio), so there was no single place to fix: `hostRt` now calls `Machine::flushOut()` on `host.evReady` (the first host call of every turn of both prelude loops) and on `host.loopWait` (before the loop sleeps), and the QuickJS loops `fflush(stdout)` at the top of each turn. `Machine::outLive` is set only by `runModule` (zinc run, AOT programs, the wasm hosts): `zinc profile`, `zinc mem` and the device core keep the program's output for themselves, unchanged. Rejected: a write per line (one `write` per console.log in loops that never yield, and three places to change); a write before every host call (it changes the stdout/stderr interleaving of goldens captured with `2>&1`). Node is the reference for the visible behaviour (a line of a running program can be read from the file). Cost: one `write` of 5 to 8 us per turn that printed (`docs/reports/zinc-next-perf.md`, ZN-638); kernels unchanged (jsonout 0.99). Checked by `tests/t0/console_live.sh` (timer and frame loops, file and pipe, the three engines; `zinc mem` still gets the whole output). Left: output printed before a blocking host call or a child process (ZN-643), and console.error / sys.exit writing the program's output to stdout under profile, mem and the device core, which existed before (ZN-644). Also from this chain, ZN-384: the WebGL limits live in `include/zn/webgl_limits.h` (a header of their own: no WebGL names in the core's `limits.h`), and the lint `shared_headers` reads a `#define` only at the start of a line, so GLSL text in strings passes and `src/gl` is no longer excluded. |
| D66 | The assets directory of a built program (ZN-314) | **A program built by `zinc build` finds its assets relative to its executable: `ZINC_ASSETS` if set (what `zinc run` does), else `assets/` beside the executable (what `zinc export` ships), else one level above it (`<project>/build/<name>`), else `assets/` of the directory it is started in; resolved once, in `assetsDir()` of `src/host/sys_host.cpp`, which every asset open and the permission exemption go through.** Before, only `ZINC_ASSETS` was read: the AOT build of examples/hero stopped with `ENOENT: open city.bin`, and the `assets/` that `zinc export` copies was never read. Scores (RULES §4, max 70): A relative to the executable then the start directory 66; B the absolute project path written into the binary at build time 52 (it breaks the byte-identical builds of ZN-333 and is wrong once the app is shipped); C assets embedded in the executable as the prototype did 52 (the right long-term answer, but it is the pack work of ZN-433 and ZN-436). Two permission guards came with it, because enforcing programs now get an assets directory: the exemption for the app's own assets no longer covers a path with a `..` component (it was a prefix comparison, so `readText('../x')` skipped `fs:read`), and an enforcing program does not take the start directory's assets. Checked: the AOT build of examples/hero, headless, 30 frames, equals the interpreter's frame (`tools/pngdiff`: 0 differing pixels); `tests/t1/aot_assets.sh`. Left: the macOS `.app` does not carry its assets (ZN-645); the exemption also covers writes (ZN-646); the guide still describes the prototype (ZN-647); Linux's `/proc/self/exe` branch waits for the cross-platform pass. Revisit when ZN-436 lands: packs replace the directory behind the same function. |
