| D67 | What zinc loads at launch (ZN-592.01) | **zinc links libSystem and libc++ only: the window host (targets/macos/hal_sdl.cpp, hal_cocoa.mm, SDL3 and the GUI frameworks) is the module `libzn_window.dylib` beside it, loaded with RTLD_GLOBAL at the first window (`src/host/window_module.cpp` forwards the `sdl_hal_*` and `hal_set_*` functions and keeps what the setters got until then); CoreFoundation is opened by the one call that needs it; zinc exports no `zn::`, harfbuzz or libc++ symbol (`tools/zinc-unexported.txt`), so dyld has no weak definition to coalesce.** Measured on the Intel Mac, `zinc --version`: 115.5M -> 31.5M instructions, 21 -> 8.3-10 ms wall (an empty program: 4.6). The module alone gave 7%: on macOS 15 libSystem already loads CoreFoundation, Foundation and ~560 images for any program, so the frameworks cost little until something links CoreFoundation, which runs ~400 initializers (Network, SkyLight: ~2 ms). The big cost was 629 weak-def coalescing binds (388 of them for the interpreter's `Machine::execT` template referenced from a table) and the libc++ weak definitions zinc exported, which made dyld patch the shared cache; no plugin or module imports a hidden symbol (checked against the imports of every cached plugin, libzn_webgl and libzn_window). Compiled programs link the window host statically (`zn_host_window`), as before; the display drivers, zinc:3d and libzn_webgl resolve SDL3 from the module, loaded before them (a plugin whose load fails on an `_SDL_` symbol is loaded again after it); `zinc fuse` carries the module inside the fused executable (trailer `ZNFUSED2`), written into the cache on the first window. Without the module a program that draws runs headless and says why. Rejected: `-no_fixup_chains` (dyld 4 binds lazy pointers at launch too: no gain), `-fvisibility=hidden` on the internal libraries (same effect as the list, many targets to touch, and libc++'s typeinfos stay default), a launch closure (dyld 4 keeps none for a command-line tool). macOS only: Linux keeps SDL linked into zinc until the cross-platform pass. Left: zinc's own ~3.5 ms (binds, 28 initializers, pages of a 12 MB binary) keep jsonout's interpreter run behind QuickJS (10.6 vs 6.9 ms best of 40): ZN-592.03. |
| D68 | What a compiled program links (ZN-592.06) | **`zinc build` links the window host (`libzn_host_window.a`, whole), SDL3 and the GUI frameworks only into programs that draw (a zinc:gfx row), use zinc:gl or call native plugins (which may call SDL3); the others get `src/host/hal_dispatch.cpp`'s weak stand-ins and never open a window. On macOS every program is linked with `-dead_strip` (meant by ZN-603 but only on arm64) and `-no_exported_symbols` (a compiled program loads no plugin; dyld then coalesces no weak definition). Fonts and images are baked only into programs that draw, in AOT and in the interpreter's cache. mimalloc skips its heap purge at exit (`MI_SKIP_COLLECT_ON_EXIT`), the VM's call stack is calloc'ed (its pages are touched as it grows).** A console program with a timer: 25 libraries -> libSystem and libc++, 3.9 -> 1.4 MB, 16.6 -> 7.0 ms on the Intel Mac (an empty program 4.9); `console.log(1)` compiled 6.6 -> 5.1 ms; `zinc run` of a cached program 5.2 -> 4.1 ms over an empty program. Rejected: dropping the window only for programs without any host call (timers, files and sockets are the console programs that matter); linking the window lazily (a static program cannot). Left: the ~240 libSystem imports the host's row dispatch pulls into every program that calls the host (ZN-592.07). |
| D69 | How M4 judges a kernel shorter than a launch (ZN-648) | **`tools/bench-m4` judges jsonout's "not slower than QuickJS" threshold on each engine's work: the cycles the process ran for the kernel minus those for an empty program of the same kind (`zinc run empty.zbc`, the compiled empty program, `qjs empty.js`; `/usr/bin/time -l` on macOS, CPU time elsewhere), measured in alternation, the fastest of each (ZN-654: medians swung 0.8-1.5x at load 8, the fastest agreed within 15%); the launches and the work are reported beside the totals, which stay in the table.** jsonout does ~1 ms of work, so its total compared zinc's 12 MB engine starting (~350 libSystem imports, ZN-592.03) with qjs, which a run-only interpreter would not close (ZN-592.05). Wall time and CPU time could not measure 1 ms of work on the development Mac while other trees build (load 7-10): the difference between two 10-20 ms medians swung from 0 to 2x; cycles agreed across runs (interpreter ~5.5M, compiled ~3.9-4.6M, QuickJS ~4.3M). jsonout is named short in the tool (its work is ~1 ms in every engine), not classified by a measured launch, which load moved too (dynsum and strings were taken for short ones). Result: the threshold now measures the interpreter's work; it was a real loss (0.77x by medians), which ZN-654 turned into 1.00-1.16x (integer strings by to_chars, one concatenation per serialised object). Rejected: dropping jsonout from the thresholds; longer inputs (the corpus outputs are frozen); one launch measured apart from the kernel runs. The engines' start-up is followed by the start-up section of the same tool and by `tests/t1/launch_time.sh`. |
| D70 | zinc:audio (ZN-390) | **zinc:audio is a plugin (`plugins/audio`) on miniaudio 0.11.25 with stb_vorbis, vendored inside the plugin (`plugins/audio/vendor/` with a `PIN` file) rather than in `third_party/`: a plugin carries what it compiles for its mirror repository and `zinc add`, as sqlite does. The engine core has no audio and a program that does not import it is unchanged (227,152 bytes either way).** API: `load`, `decode(bytes)`, `tone(hz, seconds, wave)`, a `Sound` with `play` (each call a new voice, 32 voices, the oldest one-shot gives way), `stop`, `volume`, `loop`, a master volume, and `mix(frames)` on the null backend, which is what headless and deterministic runs bind (a Zinc stand-in `native/audio.sim.ts`: nothing is built, time stands still unless `mix()` moves it). The mixer is ours and always 48 kHz stereo; the device opens on a thread, because opening Core Audio synchronously took 87 to 537 ms (`tone()` plus `play()` now return in 1.3 ms). Sounds are kept as float: miniaudio 0.11.25 drops the last frames (up to 512) of a non-float source, at a cost of 21 MiB per minute of 44.1 kHz stereo (ZN-653; reporting it upstream is the owner's step). `'noise'` uses our own xorshift because `ma_noise` white noise is all positive in that version. `load()` never throws: a bad file is a silent Sound and one warning. Size: +550 KiB on game-2d, +1.36 MiB on a command-line program; miniaudio's text is 693 KB as shipped, 477 KB at -Os. Checked on macOS only: `tests/t1/audio.sh` (mixing, volume, loop, 32 voices, resampling, WAV, FLAC, MP3, Ogg, on the interpreter and the AOT with the same 25 lines) and one real run on Core Audio that opened, played 0.26 s and closed; nobody listened. Not built: pause, seek, pitch, pan, fades, streaming (notes for ZN-440 and ZN-573). Left: Linux (ZN-651), packed apps carrying their native plugins (ZN-652). |
| D71 | How zinc:gfx draws through a 2D matrix (ZN-263.01) | **`gfx.setTransform(a, b, c, d, e, f)` (canvas order, logical pixels, after `translate`, reset each frame) turns each box command that follows (rect, rounded rect, gradient, border, shadow, image, text) into an XFORM command: its screen bounding box as the command's box, the command in its own coordinates and the matrix in the point pool. Polygons, paths and strokes are transformed point by point when recorded. The software raster draws an XFORM pixel by pixel through the inverse matrix with the distance functions of the untransformed commands (images and glyphs sampled bilinearly); the GL replay draws it with its usual paths, `quad()` taking the corners through the matrix and giving the shader the pixel's own coordinates.** Tiles, damage, culling and occlusion see a box like any other, and without a matrix nothing is recorded differently (the prototype's pixel goldens: the same 40 of 42 as before, the two known 3D differences). Rejected: a matrix in every Cmd (24 more bytes for every command of every frame), a transform state command (every pass that reads boxes would have to follow it), rendering transformed subtrees into an offscreen image (runtime images have no alpha, and text would be resampled twice), triangulating boxes into polygons (no text or images). Limits (ponytail): a clip under a matrix is the bounding box of the transformed rectangle; a transformed stroke keeps a round pen of the matrix's mean scale; the GL replay ignores the scale for edge smoothing and draws turned images without rounded corners. Checked by `tests/t1/gfx_transform.sh` (interpreter and compiled program equal to the software golden, GL within the tolerance policy). |
| D72 | glTF in zinc:3d (ZN-658, extends D25) | **`loadGltf(asset)` is a module of its own, `zinc:3d/gltf` (an alias in plugins/3d/plugin.json, file plugins/3d/gltf.ts), that reads through the three plugin's native glTF reader, not cgltf.** A native module is linked whole into every program that imports its spec (no pruning of natives), so `loadGltf` in `zinc:3d` itself would grow every 3D program; a module imported only by the programs that load models keeps the others byte-identical (measured: the same SHA-1 before and after for a zinc:3d program, +194 KB for one that imports it). The reader is the one D25 kept for three (GLB, data URIs, normalized attributes, strips and fans, embedded images), so the engine keeps one glTF reader to keep right and cgltf stays the independent check of tests/native/gltf_check.cpp. Scores (fit x3, size x2, maintainability x2, effort x1): three's reader in a separate module 5,5,5,5 = 40; a cgltf-based native module in a new plugin 5,5,3,2 = 33 (a second reader, a second image decoder, its own sim); in zinc:3d's own native module 5,1,3,3 = 26 | A model the shared reader misreads (then both loaders get the fix), or a target where three's native does not build (esp32: the 3D plugin's module stays usable without glTF) |
| D73 | Source rectangles and images from bytes in zinc:gfx (ZN-432) | **`drawImageRect(image, sx, sy, sw, sh, dx, dy, dw, dh, alpha, flags)` (flags FlipX, FlipY, Nearest) is one IMAGE command of the usual 48 bytes: the box of the whole image goes in the four fields an IMAGE did not use (`raster::image_box`, pad bit `kImageBox`), a negative size mirrors; `imageFromBytes(bytes)` decodes PNG and JPEG into a runtime image that keeps its alpha.** Rejected for the rectangle: the point pool (it would cap a frame at 8192 sprites, 256 on ESP32), a bigger command, and CLIP + IMAGE + UNCLIP (3 commands and a GL flush per sprite, no mirror). The linear path computes exactly what canvas2d's emulation computed, so `zinc:canvas`'s 9-argument `drawImage` moved onto the row with its pixels unchanged. Sampling follows the GPU, not the 2D canvas (a linear sample reads the texels around the source rectangle, clamped at the image edge; atlases extrude): software equals GL, and linear sprites differ at their edges in a browser. `imageFromBytes` uses a second static stb_image in `zn_codec` (built programs link `zn_codec`, not `zn_res` or libwebp): +50.7 KB; a file that declares more than 2^24 pixels is refused; 64 runtime images at once (ZN-670). Under `setTransform` (ZN-263.01) the box travels in the existing XFORM payload and the Cmd, with no change of either; the raster clamps the half-pixel sample of an edge to the rectangle (a turned nearest sprite otherwise showed dots of the neighbouring frame) and smooths the edge, the GL replay cuts it (ZN-678). The appended gfx rows are one list, `isGfxTailRow`, read by `isSysRow` and by QuickJS's `installHost`, which so gained setTransform, resetTransform, onResize, commandCount and commandsFree. canvas2d behaviour changes that no golden covered: `imageSmoothingEnabled = false` samples nearest on baked images too; a 1:1 source rectangle of a runtime image at a fractional position is blended, not row-copied. Checked: `image_rect` (sprite sheet, flips, alpha, edge cut, the same hash on the interpreter, the AOT and QuickJS; 10 sprites through a matrix), `image_rect_gl`, `image_rect_wasm` (headless Chrome, nearest sprites exact, game-2d's sprites in the export), the pixel canary, `gfx_transform` unchanged, `proto_devtools` and a prototype run of examples/canvas/sketch. Browser host: `imageFromBytes`, baked fonts, gradients, clip, translate and setTransform are not done (ZN-672, ZN-673). Numbers: `docs/reports/zinc-next-perf.md`, ZN-432. |
| D74 | The museum table: project format, light table and object viewer (ZN-650.01 to .04) | **`examples/museum-table` is a player: everything it shows comes from one project file (JSON, version 1; `src/project.ts` reads, validates with messages naming the field, copies and writes it; load then save is byte-identical), in the manner of Klynt: a media library (photo, model, panorama, text, audio, video, each with texts by language and a required credit), sequences holding items in space and time, links (a button, or a hotspot on an item), presentations, storyboard positions (x, y is the centre of a 240 by 150 node), and the table, kiosk, accessibility and theme settings the owner asked for.** Photo cards are plain zinc:gfx draws under `setTransform` (ZN-263.01), not textured quads in a zinc:3d scene (the first version: nearest texels, no alpha, one repainted image per card, the 64-image limit): anti-aliased edges, real shadows, text sharp at every zoom, 150 lines less; the cost is the software raster (115 ms for 8 cards, captures only, ZN-680). Gestures are the table's own code, not `plugins/gestures`, which has no per-target finger ownership (ZN-684): each gesture owns its card, and a finger added or lifted moves it by less than a unit (the test refuses a step over 16 units, 3 degrees or 6% of scale). In the object viewer the object turns and the camera and lights stay; a view is yaw, pitch and a distance in radii, field of view 40 degrees; the turning, inertia and glide are `src/orbit.ts`, which the panorama view of ZN-650.09 extends. Tests compare frame hashes and logs, not PNG files. The sample, "floating-world", is real and CC0: nine photographs from The Met, three from the Art Institute of Chicago, a Kangxi vase of the Smithsonian (decimated from 150,000 to 12,000 triangles with the vendored meshoptimizer; the OBJ ships because the 150k GLB would take the example to 11.4 MB, ZN-681), a Poly Haven panorama; 7.1 MB in all; works the Met's API does not mark public domain were left out. Measured in a window on the GPU: 30 moving cards at 60 fps at 1920x1080 (114 to 118 without vsync), 40 fps at 3104x1746 (ZN-679); the object draws in 0.7 ms (OBJ) or 1.4 ms (150k GLB). Not verified: no touch hardware (scripted touches only); the French and English texts were written from the museums' records and not re-read by a curator; nothing generated by `say` is committed (the narration will use an open voice, ZN-650.05). |
| D75 | More than one window (ZN-663) | **A window beside the main one draws a slice of the frame's command list into pixels of its own, on the CPU, shown by its own SDL_Renderer; zinc:ui exchanges its per-surface state around that window's frame.** The runtime records the window's commands between `beginWindow` and `endWindow` like `beginImage` (physical pixels at the main window's scale), rasterizes them only when their hash changed (`raster::frame_hash`), hands the pixels to the HAL and drops them from the main list; while the callback runs, the input, `width()`, `height()` and `keep()` are the window's. The HAL contract is six optional calls (`hal_win_*`, weak defaults: main window only); the SDL HAL routes events by window id, hides a closed main window while others are open and ends the program with the last one; the headless HAL plays `window N` / `close` script events. zinc:ui keeps one set of module variables (the code reads one surface) and swaps the ~70 interaction variables with a `Surface` object around a window's frame; the clock, animations and scrolls step once per engine frame, and a change made while one surface draws marks the others dirty from outside (`extLayout`, `extPaint`), which never marks back, so two idle windows stay idle. Scores (fit x3, effort x2, risk x2, performance x2, maintainability x2): CPU slice per window 4,4,4,3,4 = 46 (the GL replay and the damage diff stay single-window; a 1280 x 960 window repaint costs a full raster when it changes); a per-window context of the whole runtime (command buffers, damage, GL replay, HAL state as objects) 5,1,2,5,4 = 39 (every global of gfx.cpp, gl_replay.cpp and hal_sdl.cpp moves: the shared core, weeks); zinc:ui rewritten around a surface object 5,1,1,4,5 = 37 (thousands of references in lib/std/ui.ts, shared with the prototype) | A window whose CPU raster shows in profiles (then the GL replay per window), or zinc:gl / QuickJS programs that need a second window |
| D76 | The museum table's storyboard editor (ZN-650.07, slices 1 and 2) | **The editor is an application of its own, `examples/museum-table/editor/` (a zinc.json has one entry), a desktop tool for mouse and keyboard that imports the player's format module and writes exactly `serializeProject`'s text.** Every operation leaves a project `parseProject` accepts, so Save never writes a file that cannot be reopened: deleting a sequence removes or retargets the links and points that led to it in the same undo step (a dialog gives the count and offers another target). Undo keeps a `copyProject` per step and merges typing; "unsaved" compares bytes; 50 mixed operations undone and redone give back every file byte for byte. An id derived from a title follows it while it still matches. The storyboard draws a sequence as a 240 by 150 node centred on its x, y, zooms by layout in four steps (zinc:ui paints no transform), and a link shows a dot that opens into its label. The owner's answers: guided by default for museum staff, with an advanced mode (Cmd+E) that is a view over the same commands (exact numbers, the selection as the file has it, the key of every command); the interface itself in French and English from one string table (`strings.ts`, typed keys), apart from the language switch of the content. Thumbnails come from the project folder through `readBytes` and `imageFromBytes` (one decode per frame, 320 px, 40 kept: the 64 runtime images limit, ZN-670). Workarounds marked in the code: a constant of Latin characters until fonts at run time (ZN-669), the shell's PWD and LANG until zinc:sys gives the launch directory and the locale (ZN-687). Checked by `tests/t1/museum_editor.sh` (scripted sessions building projects from empty, file and frame goldens); never run in a real window. Left as subtasks: settings forms (ZN-650.07.01), points of interest on a model and in a panorama (.02), preview by embedding the player (.03), narration, sound and film (.04), tools for agencies (.05). |
| D77 | zinc:physics3d on Jolt and the 3D pinball (ZN-583.02, ZN-649.01, ZN-649.02) | **The pinball `examples/pinball3d` plays a table file in real 3D with complete 3D physics, as the owner asked: `zinc:physics3d` (`plugins/physics3d`, Jolt Physics 5.6.0, MIT, vendored in the plugin as four unity files with its pin and checksum) scored 62 against Box2D in the table plane 58, Bullet 46 and hand-written physics 43 (RULES §4).** The plugin: a typed API (World, Shape sphere / box / capsule / cylinder / convex hull / mesh / height field, bodies with plain fields read in bulk after each step, hinge and slider with motors, contacts with the approach speed, sensors, ray and shape casts, layers, save and restore of the state), one thread, no clock, the same calls on the interpreter, the AOT and QuickJS through a generic native bridge (`src/qjs/native_js.cpp`: plugin `zinc:` specifiers resolve under QuickJS), deterministic (600 steps replayed identically from the start and from a saved state on the three engines). A fast ball: 10,000 scripted shots (27 mm, 3 to 20 m/s, 240 Hz) and none through a wall, a ramp, a flipper or another ball, under two documented conditions: a kinematic flipper needs 4 collision steps (960 Hz), and two fast balls head-on need collision steps = ceil(closing speed x dt / (0.5 x radius)), because Jolt sweeps a body against where the other was. Cost: 1.92 MB in a program that imports it, nothing in one that does not; cold plugin build 48 s, a wrapper edit 6.7 s. The table format is one schema (`examples/pinball3d/src/format`) that gives the validation with messages naming the field, the canonical text (load then save is byte-identical), the printed reference `docs/pinball-table-format.md` and, later, the editor's forms; one list per element kind; ramps, rails and upper playfields are 3D paths with heights; a package (`.zpinball`) is the JSON with its assets in base64. The game is a library an editor can embed (`src/game.ts`: Game, placeBall, show(scene)); flippers and plunger are kinematic bodies at 960 Hz; switches are lines tested in the game (Jolt sensors are not swept); both layouts, landscape and portrait, are data. First table: "Tidal Lock", original (ramp with a wire rail, dock, bumpers, missions and ranks, multiball, ball save, tilt, high scores). Checked: `physics3d`, `pinball3d_format` (16 broken files, each message naming its field), `pinball3d` and `pinball3d_aot` (a scripted 60 s game with the same 203-line log on the interpreter in both layouts and compiled; six pixel goldens exact on the software renderer; the GPU frame held to SSIM 0.95 since zinc:3d multisamples by default: antialiased edges are not the software frame's). Not met or not shown: the 3 ms step for 1,000 bodies (4.1 to 4.5 ms on a loaded, heat-throttled machine, Jolt's own step being 4.3 ms: ZN-690), 120 fps (a 60 Hz display; physics and rules 0.3 ms, scene and panel 0.6 to 0.8 ms per frame), an upper playfield on Tidal Lock, textures and modelled pieces (ZN-649.08), sound (ZN-649.03); no human has judged the feel. |
| D78 | Fonts at run time (ZN-669, revisits D45) | **The runtime rasterizer (runtime/ttf.cpp) is the baker's algorithm (src/res/res.cpp, the prototype's resources.ts): contours in font units, quadratics flattened into 8 segments, composites by offset and x/y scale, nonzero winding sampled 4 x 4 per pixel in doubles without fused multiply-adds (pragma in ttf.cpp; plugins already use -ffp-contract=off). A glyph rasterized at run time is the baked glyph of that size, so `gfx.loadFont(file, px)` (a baked family or a .ttf of the assets, read at run time when the baker did not embed it) draws exactly like a size written in the program, and a baked font that lacks a character hands the text to its twin rasterized from the same file (raster::text_font). A program whose own files have a text field embeds its fonts with every character they have (hb-subset to the whole cmap, still without hinting or layout tables).** D45's alternative of an exact-size `gfx.font` stays rejected (the prototype answers the nearest baked size); `loadFont` is the explicit call. Measured: run time vs baked frame identical (tests/t1/fonts_runtime.sh); first measure of 37 glyphs 0.6 ms at 24 px, 3 ms at 96 px (17 to 85 us per glyph), then 1 us; programs without fields bake byte-identical resources, with fields +366 to +387 KB (hero, forms); bouncing-ball's binary +24 bytes; HiDPI text changes by up to 35 levels on antialiased edges (scale_push re-recorded). Scores (fit x3, pixels x3, effort x2, size x2): the baker's algorithm at run time 5,5,4,4 = 46; keep the signed-area rasterizer and bake a ladder of sizes 2,3,5,2 = 33 (what the museum table did); full fonts for every program 5,5,5,1 = 42 | Glyph rasterization at large sizes in profiles (then an active-edge table in cover()), or a program that types characters its fonts do not have (then the system fallback of D33 for the glyph tables) |
| D79 | The museum table: sequences, narration and the table mode (ZN-650.05, ZN-650.06) | **The player now plays the Klynt part of a project: timed items, links, hotspots, overlays and `next`; a storyboard view with the visited trail; guided presentations with narration and subtitles; and a table mode for one or two visitors sharing one story. Narration is synthesised with Piper 2023.11.14-2 (MIT) and two voices whose model cards allow distribution: French `fr_FR-mls-medium`, speaker 1840 (trained from scratch on Multilingual LibriSpeech, CC BY 4.0), English `en_GB-cori-medium` (trained from scratch on LibriVox recordings, public domain); refused: every voice fine-tuned from `en_US-lessac` (data licensed to a named licensee), from `en_US-ryan` (CC BY-NC-SA) and `fr_FR-tom` (AGPL).** The tool lives outside the repository (`~/.cache/zinc-museum-tts`); `tools/narration.sh` regenerates the 8 clips (MP3, 533 kB) and their WebVTT cues from the transcripts of the project; nothing made with the macOS `say` command is committed (the owner's decision after the licence concern). A narration is said once per visit and always in a presentation; its length comes from the cues, not from the decoded sound, so headless runs are exact. A fade is a dip to the table's colour; `next` waits for free hands. Table mode: one frame for everything, turned toward a side by a tap or press within 15% of that edge (guessing the side from every touch turned the table under a visitor); photo cards stay free; an attract loop after 60 s and a reset after 120 s, both in the project file, and the reset is checked to give the frame of a fresh start; the attract loop is silent; a faulty project is reported on the error output and never drawn to a visitor; `kiosk.fullscreen` takes effect with `ZINC_KIOSK=1` so a developer is not trapped. No field was added to the format; two meanings were made precise in the reference (a hotspot's fractions are of the picture for a photo or a film; an overlay closes by a link or `next` to the sequence underneath). zinc:gfx `setTransform` was enough for the turned panels. Checked by `museum_visit` (a scripted visit: three links, the storyboard, a jump, a presentation, the language switch, on the interpreter and compiled) and `museum_mode` (four sides, two visitors taking turns, idle and reset). The example is 8.0 MB (limit raised to 12 MB). Short of the brief: a paused presentation restarts its step because zinc:audio cannot pause or seek (ZN-695); the film shows its poster (ZN-650.08); smaller things in ZN-650.13. Not verified: nobody listened to the narration (pronunciation of ukiyo-e, Ninomaru, Van Gogh), no sound went through Core Audio, no window was opened, no touch hardware. |
| D80 | The pinball's sound, look and initials (ZN-649.03, ZN-649.08, ZN-649.09) | **Every sound, image, model and light of Tidal Lock is named in its table file and made by a script kept in the example; the game stays a silent library that leaves cues, and `TableAudio` plays them.** Sound: 40 MP3 files, 595 kB (64 kbit/s mono through LAME: within the 1 MB budget, a mature encoder on the machine, and loops without a gap measured through zinc:audio). The mechanical sounds are cut from six recordings under CC0 1.0 on freesound.org, each licence read on its own page on 2026-10-10 (a 1977 Gottlieb machine by schafferdavid: flippers, bumpers, plunger, drain; a marble on a table by NeatoEnt: the roll, a drop, a hole), recorded in `LICENSES.md` and in the table's `credits`; the other 28 (effects, 11 jingles, 4 pieces of music, one per game state, cross-faded in 0.4 s) are synthesised by `tools/tidal_lock_sounds.py`. Because the game only leaves cues, a scripted game has a sound log (`pinball3d_sound`, the same on the interpreter and compiled; renaming a sound in the table changes what plays with no code change). The rolling sound is one loop whose volume and rate follow the fastest ball. Look: a painted playfield and decals laid flat from above, modelled pieces through OBJ files (the model path now runs), a cabinet, a backglass, and an upper playfield at 5.5 cm with its own flipper, all drawn by `tools/tidal_lock_art.py` from the table file (14 images, 4 models, 679 kB). zinc:3d has neither blending nor a light per piece, so a pool of light is an opaque patch of a second, lit painting that matches the first at its rim: pools are on the playfield only and must not overlap (ZN-696). Jolt's results depend on the order bodies are made in: the deck's bodies moved a bumper hit by 1 ms, so `physics.ts` makes upper-level bodies after the playfield's and the balls, and the scripted log is the old one plus the deck flipper's lines. Two standups were moved rather than a guide added (a new playfield-level body would have changed the scripted log). Initials are entered with the game's own controls; `--scores=<file>` keeps the table of a deterministic run or of a cabinet in a known file. The schema gained what the editor asked for: `Field.role` (point, path, area, direction, profile, rotation, offset), `Field.unit`, `checkTable`. Frame in a window (2560x1600 physical, GPU, load 4.4 to 5.2): physics and rules 0.35 to 0.42 ms, scene and panel 1.3 to 1.8 ms compiled (0.6 to 0.8 before the look), 2.0 to 2.3 ms on the interpreter, both at the display's 60 Hz. Not verified: nobody listened to the sounds or played the table (ZN-649.12); the scores' storage path between two real runs; a `.zpinball` package carrying sounds, images and models. |
| D81 | The pinball table editor (ZN-649.04, ZN-649.06) | **The editor is an application of its own, `examples/pinball3d-editor/`, that embeds the game as a library (Game, TableScene, TableAudio, View: nothing rewritten) and whose forms are GENERATED from the table format's schema: a field added to the format appears in the editor without editor code.** The document is the format's own tree; every operation is addressed by path and checked by the game's loader (`checkTable`) before it replaces the document, so Save never writes a table the game refuses; a rename or a deletion is mended from the loader's own fault messages rather than from a copy of its rules; undo keeps the earlier trees (50 mixed operations undone and redone give every file byte for byte); a point drag edits the document live as one undo step (8.3 ms per edit on the 17 KB table). The schema says what a field is for: `Field.role` (point, path, area, direction, profile, rotation, offset, level) and `Field.unit`, so the top view moves, turns, mirrors and draws any element without knowing its kind (mirrored copies of Tidal Lock's left guide, slingshot and flipper are byte for byte its right ones). Play in the editor is the real game: a ball dropped at a point gives line for line what the game's probe prints for the same drop; the game's own window opens beside (several windows, ZN-663); sounds are heard through TableAudio; rules and missions are edited as forms of the same generic kind (a rule made in the editor adds its 300 points to a bumper in the game's probe). The owner's answers: guided by default with an advanced mode over the same commands, the interface in French and English, work in a folder and export one package (`.zpinball`: Tidal Lock with its sounds, images and models plays the scripted game from the package with the same log). Tests reach things by key, path or name (Cmd+F a field, Cmd+G a thing or `+kind` to add, Cmd+J the file's text), never by the position of a line, so the format can grow; frames are compared by bytes, else on the top view's crop (`tools/pngdiff --crop`), the form's pixels on a fixture schema, 3D frames by SSIM. A table made from nothing by a scripted session plays the game's scripted game with a score (81,500) and is the start of the second table (ZN-649.05). Short of a non-coder's needs: file fields are typed paths, colours are hex, rules are forms rather than sentences, the playfield painting is made outside the editor; a package altered after export is not refused at load (no hash function in the standard modules, ZN-698). Not verified: a sound heard, the windows on a real screen, any human use. |
| D82 | Goldens recorded on Apple Silicon, checked on other machines (ZN-612) | **Each comparison gets the measure that fits what differs, chosen so that the Intel Mac's honest differences pass and a real regression fails.** The 3D example frames (three_3d) differ by an edge pixel (3d-model: 3 pixels, max 61) or a rounding of 1 (three-cubes: 5 pixels) although every host, plugin and AOT unit is built with `-ffp-contract=off`: the remaining source is the platform libm under `Math.sin`/`cos` (Apple's arm64 and x86_64 libm differ in last bits). They compare within 1 per channel and 8 pixels (`pngdiff --tol 1 --max-diff 8`): a shape moved by a pixel (15739 pixels) or a colour changed by 3 (every pixel) fails. The cause is filed rather than hidden: ZN-703 routes Math through fdlibm, as V8 and so the sim do, after which the frames can compare exactly again. The video's first frame (video) is a hardware decoder's output through FFmpeg's SIMD colour conversion (x86 and NEON paths are not bit exact; `SWS_BITEXACT` would cost the C path at playback): it compares on the picture (`--crop 0,0,192,108`) by PSNR ≥ 30 dB and mean error ≤ 4 (new `pngdiff --psnr`/`--mae`): the Intel decode scores 32.2 dB / 2.3, while a one-pixel shift (22.8 dB), swapped channels (16.9), a brightness off by 6 (mae 5.8), a limited/full range mistake (28.3), BT.601 read as 709 (29.5) and nearest chroma (26.8) fail. The WebGL conformance gate keeps one pass list and adds, per GPU family (the unmasked vendor of the context the pages get: apple, amd, intel...), the pages that family's driver is known to fail (`tests/golden/webgl/conformance-known-amd.txt`, page and why): they print KNOWN, every other page of the pass list still fails the gate. Of the five AMD pages two are ours, not the driver's (WebGL must zero what GL leaves undefined: ZN-701) and three are driver bugs browsers rewrite around (ZN-702); two CPU-heavy pages that timed out under load (59 s and 12 s alone on the Intel Mac) got longer hang guards instead of list entries. Rejected: one tolerance for every golden (it would hide regressions where exact output is the norm), re-recording the goldens on the Intel Mac (they would then fail on Apple Silicon, where they were made). |
| D83 | The web environment for JavaScript libraries on QuickJS (ZN-566, ZN-568) | **A thin environment of our own, `lib/std/webenv.js` (67 KB of JavaScript read at run time, so zinc carries no byte of it, +14.5 KB for its native side `src/qjs/webenv.cpp`), off by default (zinc.json `web`), written against what the game libraries actually call: `window`, `document` with the elements they create, events and listeners, `requestAnimationFrame` on the engine's frame loop, timers, pointer and key input, `fetch` / XHR / URL / Blob on the host's network and files, `localStorage`, `navigator`, `performance`, and a canvas element whose `getContext('webgl' | 'webgl2')` is the existing WebGL binding.** Scores (RULES §4): a thin environment of our own 67; the same with proven parsers where the spec is a parser 64 (its rule is applied: URL is the WHATWG parser of `lib/std/web.ts`, structuredClone is QuickJS's serializer, DOMParser waits for a vendored XML parser, ZN-706); a DOM library in JavaScript 43 (only linkedom runs on QuickJS: 411 KB, 19 to 21 ms, +5.2 MB, and it lacks rAF, fetch, input events, layout and canvas contexts). To load it the type stripper of the QuickJS engine keeps `static` and blanks Zinc decorators. Engine robustness that real libraries needed (ZN-566): the stack limit follows the thread's real stack less 1 MiB, capped at 8 MiB, so deep recursion is a catchable RangeError (depth 6,837 on the default stack) instead of a SIGSEGV; an unhandled promise rejection prints `Uncaught (in promise)` with its stack and dispatches `unhandledrejection`, and without a web environment ends the program with 101, as the typed engine and Node do; `onFrame` registered after an awaited timer receives frames. Booting unmodified, each with a T1 test: p5.js 1.11.13 (WEBGL mode), three.js r186 with OrbitControls, PixiJS 7.4.3; not yet: PixiJS 8.22 (`Vertex Array Objects are not supported`) and Phaser 3.90 (`Invalid call of 'texture'`), both from WebGL binding defects of ZN-565, then image loading (ZN-569); `getContext('2d')` draws nothing until ZN-570. p5.js is LGPL-2.1 and is NOT in the repository (RULES §3 keeps LGPL out of the tree): `tools/test-lib p5-1.11.13` takes it from its npm release into `~/.zinc/cache/test-libs/`, checked against a pinned sha256, and `webenv_p5` is skipped when it is neither cached nor downloadable; PixiJS and three.js (MIT) are vendored. Cost: +6.5 ms to create a context with the environment on (10.2 against 16.7 ms, nearly all of it parsing the JavaScript: a bytecode cache, ZN-575, would remove most); first frame 320 to 420 ms after the context for the three libraries, frame 0.3 to 0.8 ms on their minimal scenes (load 4.4 to 5.5). Not verified: a WebGL canvas in a real window (the live path ran on SDL's dummy driver only), the `resize` event, https and permissions with fetch. |
| D84 | The interpreter's debugger (ZN-371.01) | **A debug build, not a debug mode of the normal one: `zinc debug` lowers the program with a `DbgLine` at each statement and a `DbgVar` after each write of a local, skips inlining and recursion unrolling (every call keeps its frame), and the ZBC emitter turns them into a `Line` instruction (24-bit index into the module's line table: file, line, column) and a move into the local's home register (one per named local, right after the parameters, below every call window; a reference there is retained, the previous one released, and every home released at return).** The debugger stops at a `Line` whose byte it set (breakpoints set the bytes of their line, stepping sets them all, a pause request would too) and walks the interpreter's frame stack for the call stack; the depth decides step over / out. Locals are read from the homes, formatted from the variable's IR type (numbers, booleans, strings, arrays, objects by the field names a debug build keeps, a cell's value). Why homes rather than tracking SSA values: the optimiser and the register allocator are untouched, the value of a variable is in one place at every statement, and the retain keeps a reference alive however early the RC pass releases its SSA value (a leak only when an exception unwinds the frame, in debug builds). The cost is all in the debug build: normal builds are byte-identical (59 programs compared) and the interpreter runs them in a copy of its loop whose `Line` handler is empty — the first version, one loop with a never-taken call to the hook, ran every program 6-7% slower (fib, nbody, instructions retired): a call anywhere in the computed-goto loop changes the register allocation of all of it, cold attribute or not; the copy costs 73 KB of binary. A debug build runs 1.4x (nbody) to 2.1x (fib, inlining and unrolling off) the instructions of a normal one, the same with the debugger idle or armed on a line not reached. The engine's own code (the prelude, `zinc:*` modules, `lib/std`, `lib/compat`, plugins) gets no lines or homes: it runs without stops and stays out of the stack. Debug modules are never encoded (the line table and the homes live only in memory); the verifier refuses a `Line` index past the table. Front ends: the command line now; DAP (VS Code) and CDP (Chrome's Sources panel) are ZN-371.02 and .04 on the same core. |
| D85 | The museum editor: preview, points of interest, project settings (ZN-650.07.03, .02, .01) | **The player became a class the editor embeds (`examples/museum-table/src/player.ts`: `Player(project, firstSequenceId, lang)` with `frame(dt, x, y, w, h)`; `main.ts` is only the window, the command line and `onFrame`), its files come through functions the host replaces (`src/files.ts`: the editor points them at the project folder, the table at its assets), and whoever owns the window clears it.** Preview: "Play from here" runs the real player on the unsaved project, in the editor or in a second window (ZN-663) that follows the sequence being edited 0.25 s after the last change; the file on disk is untouched by a preview. Points of interest: a click on the model or in the panorama is turned into a place by `ObjectView.ray` against the OBJ's triangles or `PanoramaView.unproject`, kept with four digits, a view in whole degrees; the drawn position matches the click within 1.5 pixel in the test; a glTF or GLB model has no triangles in the editor, so its point goes on the plane through the middle of the object (zinc:3d has no ray cast, ZN-683). Items on the table are drawn by one zinc:gfx function under a matrix at every angle, and turned by a handle. Settings: forms with a live sample that is the real player; removing a language removes its texts in the same undo step; a shorter reach moves the buttons back within it; the preview buttons sit at the corner of each view and the settings open from the project's name, because the top bar has no room left in French at 1440. The format gained no field (two exports, `mediaJson` and `sequenceJson`). The three criteria of ZN-650.07 are met by scripted sessions that build three sequences from an empty project with media, texts in two languages, links and points; left: narration, sound and film (.04), tools for agencies (.05). Short of the brief: fonts and logo are plain fields because the table does not apply them yet; text sizes are kept but the player cannot show them (ZN-650.10); the default language cannot be changed. Not verified: a real window (the second window on the GPU, a narration heard); other CPUs (the points' coordinates and three frames depend on sin and cos and are exact on this Mac, see D82); a glTF model in the points editor. |
| D86 | The pinball: pools of light, the editor's files and looks, a second table (ZN-649.10, .13, .15, .05) | **A second table, "Twilight Fair" (a funfair at dusk, chosen over the abyss because it opposes Tidal Lock in colour and sound and its strings of bulbs use overlapping pools of light), was made with the editor only: `assets/tables/twilight-fair/table.json` is the byte-for-byte result of five scripted editor sessions kept in the repository (`tools/twilight_fair/sessions`, replayed by the test `pinball3d_second_table`), and its commit holds no game or editor code.** What the editor could not do, which is the honest measure of it: two rules with a condition (all lane lights on; all coconuts down) had to be written in the advanced field as the file has it, because the form cannot build a `lights` or `targets` condition one field at a time (every intermediate state is refused, ZN-649.14); off-grid places, angles and the kicker's exit were typed in forms rather than pointed at; the painting (11 images), 2 models and 34 synthesised sounds were made by scripts outside the editor (715 KB). The scripted game runs on both tables (74,410 on the new one, a mission done). Pools of light are additive meshes cut to their level (zinc:3d blending, ZN-696), so they overlap and exist on the upper playfield; Tidal Lock is migrated (its second painting is gone, 208 KB less) and `look.textureLit` still works for a table that names one. The rules hear a bump of the table and a letter signed. In the editor: a file field is a chooser of the folder's files that knows its kind (`Schema.asset(name, kind, doc)`: image, sound, model), imports go to `art/` and `sounds/` and ask before replacing, refresh is a command and not a watcher, unused files are listed; a colour is picked in a square with the table's palette, a light's glow by a handle that comes from the role `radius` and not from the field's name, the panel's items are dragged in each layout, the camera is set in the 3D pane. Two editor defects found by making the table were fixed (a click picked the wrong line of a filtered list). Not verified: nobody played either table or heard them (ZN-649.12); the real file dialog and a drag from the Finder; a file changed by another program. |
| D87 | The debugger's expressions and exceptions (ZN-371.05) | **Watches, hovers, breakpoint conditions and logpoint messages are evaluated by the debugger itself, not compiled into the program: the frontend's parser reads the text, names resolve to the paused frame's locals, its arrow function's captures and the program's globals, and the operations are typed by the declarations a debug build keeps (IR type names of locals, fields and globals), so the errors are the checker's own: Z0101 unknown name, Z0106 no such property, Z0107 operator on these types; a literal is a `number` as the checker types it.** Why not the checker on a snippet: it needs the whole program (re-checked at every stop, 0.1-0.8 s), and running the result would mean loading code into a machine whose class table is fixed. What it covers: members (declared class first, `.length`, `.size`), indexing of arrays and strings, unary, arithmetic, bitwise, comparison, equality, `&&` `||` `??`, `?:`, `as`, `!`. What it refuses: calls and assignments (a watch never changes the program), templates. Conditions use JavaScript truthiness; an error in a condition stops with the error; a logpoint's `{expression}` parts are evaluated and its message goes to the front end (stdout, a DAP output event, `Runtime.consoleAPICalled`), never a stop. Exceptions: the debugger's copy of the interpreter loop calls the debugger before a `Throw` unwinds; it walks the frames' handlers as the unwinding would (without unwinding) to tell caught from uncaught, and stops on uncaught or on every throw as asked (`catch`, DAP exception filters, `Debugger.setPauseOnExceptions`); normal runs never reach it. Debug builds now keep field type names and the program's own globals (names and types; the engine's are left out), and arrow functions' captures show as a Closure scope. |
| D88 | `// @ts-ignore` (ZN-155, the parity gate) | **Not honoured: the diagnostic stays an error.** Zinc Next compiles the checked types into its code (typed registers, the AOT's C++): a type error that is ignored leaves nothing correct to run, where the prototype's simulator ran the program as JavaScript and its C++ build failed as here. Options scored: honour it only on the simulator profile (fit 2: a program that runs in one place and not in another; effort 4) 34; turn the line into Dyn values (fit 3, performance 2, maintainability 2) 38; refuse with the error (fit 4: the same result on every engine and target, maintainability 5, effort 5) 52. `tools/parity-audit` lists the row as BY-DECISION. |
| D89 | The ZPK pack format (ZN-433) | **ZPK, an indexed binary stored inside the .zapp** (report games/toolchain-assets-loading.md 6.1: ZPK 66, ustar plus an index 60, zip through miniz 53, PhysicsFS 48). 32-byte header (ZPK1, version, flags, count, alignment, offsets and sizes of the TOC, names and zstd dictionary); TOC rows of 64 B, one cache line (u64 offset, stored size and size, group, kind, codec, 16 B of the SHA-256 of the stored bytes, checked before a decoder sees them), sorted by FNV-1a 64 of the name then the name bytes; data in (group, name) order, aligned per profile (16 B by default, a power of two up to 1 MiB). Codecs none, lz4 (hc 12), zstd (19) and zstd with a dictionary trained single-threaded (deterministic); an entry that does not shrink is stored as is; the same inputs give the same bytes in any order. `Reader::indexHash()` (header, TOC, names, dictionary) is what a signed manifest records; per-entry hashes give integrity only. lz4 1.10.0 and zstd 1.5.7 vendored, zstd without its assembler. Revisit: a faster SHA-256 if first reads show in launch times; zstd's size macros where its decoder is too big (`docs/zpk.md`). |
| D90 | UI transitions (ZN-274) | **No record per node:** each writer of a style (rebuildStyle, syncState, applyProp) notes what the properties it changes show and starts, retargets or skips their tweens at once; running tweens live in `anims` (52 B more per transitioning node, where a per-node record cost about 0.8 KB, 2.4 KB for `transition-all`). **A budget of 8 tweens at once** on heaps of 256 KiB or less (esp32, ps1), shared by transitions and `animate()`; past it a change takes its end at once. **`duration-N` alone transitions every property**, as CSS's initial `transition-property: all`; the default curve is Tailwind's cubic-bezier(0.4, 0, 0.2, 1). **Colours ease at paint time**, channel by channel in i32 (fx12-safe), from the colour shown when the target changes; the opaque `hover:/focus:/active:` text and border colours still switch at once. **A node's first style does not transition**, as in CSS. The 'record nothing per frame' half of ZN-274's criterion 2 moved to ZN-364.01 (review of 2026-10-10, RULES §6). |
| D91 | How asset importers run (ZN-434) | **Pinned upstream CLI tools as subprocesses, plus importers written in Zinc** (report games/toolchain-assets-loading.md section 9, scored per RULES §4: 66, against in-process importers behind a C table 55 and everything as Zinc scripts 45). The built-in copy, image and font-baker importers stay in-process in the CLI. Artifacts are cached by an XXH3-128 key (xxHash 0.8.4) of the source bytes, the importer and its options and the engine's executable stamp, in ~/.zinc/cache/assets (trimmed LRU to 2 GiB); each group becomes a ZPK pack (D89) rewritten only when its key changes. Revisit if spawning costs show on many small imports. |
| D92 | zinc:assets v2 at run time (ZN-436) | **Groups are the `<group>.zpk` packs of the asset build (D91), mapped in place and searched in order;** `assets.mount()` puts a pack in front (mods, patches), overlays apply per entry name. **Handles are counted and need an explicit `release()`** (Zinc has no finalizers for plain objects). **Two queues (load, prefetch) with promotion**, four jobs at a time on the libuv pool, each with a pooled zstd context; one turn per frame makes finished jobs resident in queue order up to 4 MB (`ZINC_ASSET_TURN`); a deterministic run waits for them, and one-core targets decode in the turn, sliced by bytes rather than by a clock so runs stay deterministic. **The ram budget in force is enforced everywhere** (the 1 GB desktop default included; a project raises it in zinc.json): `loadGroup` refuses a group that would cross it, naming both. `zinc run` builds and mounts dev packs only for projects that declare `assets.groups`, so existing demos run as before. |
| D93 | UI gradients (ZN-261) | **One `gradientPaint` command, a rounded box (RECT) carrying the canvas2d paint record** (linear, CSS-ellipse radial, conic, +8 repeating, N stops), not a POLY with the canvas paint: the edges get the coverage of every other box, one quad on the GPU instead of stencil passes, and the record, `paint_at` and the shader are canvas2d's. **The GPU computes up to 8 stops analytically in the shader** (scored 62 against a 1x256 ramp texture page 55: no texture page, the raster's rounding); more stops come from a CPU tile. An axis gradient whose stops share one alpha is drawn as chained two-stop rects with seams on whole pixels (the old from/to command unchanged). **Colours interpolate in sRGB**, CSS's default (Tailwind v4 asks oklab: later.md). **A missing from-/to- stop is transparent**, as in Tailwind. Below T2 (esp32, ps1, ps2) two stops along an axis, below T3 a conic is its stops' average, each fallback logged once. |
| D94 | Images in a box: object-fit, background images, tint (ZN-262) | **One painter for `<image>` fit and background images, in lib/std/ui.ts only:** each tile is one `drawImageRect` of the visible part of the image, edges on whole pixels (no seams), a clip only for a rounded box; a plain `<image>` keeps the prototype's single drawImage command. **The tint multiplies** (drawImageRect's multiply, ZN-581), as the criterion asks; React Native's `tintColor` replaces opaque pixels instead, and the two agree on white and grey template icons (docs say so); the class is twrnc's `tint-*` (Tailwind has none). **Tile budget:** 16 tiles on T0, 64 on T1, 4096 above and never more than `commandsFree() - 2`; past it, fewer larger tiles laid over the box, logged once. The box is both positioning and painting area (borders are paint-only) with its single radius; an image and a gradient on one node paint the gradient over the image (CSS's scrim over a photo). |
| D95 | Sprite atlases (ZN-438) | **An `"as": "sprite"` rule packs PNG, JPEG, WebP, SVG, `.aseprite` (cute_aseprite 1.04) and TexturePacker or Aseprite sheets into atlas pages with stb_rect_pack 1.01; the atlas is assembled when its pack is written, from per-file cached frame artifacts named relative to their file (a cache key covers bytes, not paths); pages keep straight alpha; the frame table is text, read by the pure-Zinc `zinc:sprite`; trim keeps one transparent texel and extrusion is counted in points, so fractional positions draw exactly the loose image; `image()` of zinc:gfx finds pack images and `zinc:sprite` falls back to the loose files without packs, so no new host API.** Straight against premultiplied alpha: premultiplied rgba8 changes translucent texels, which breaks pixel identity with the loose images (criterion 2); the GL replay already blends straight alpha. Text table against a binary one: the table is small, read once, and parsed in Zinc on every engine without a host row. stb_image_resize2 (named by the task) is not vendored: nothing in an atlas resamples. | ZN-439 (mipmaps, rgb565/rgba4444 pages) asks for resampling or premultiplied pages; a sheet where the text table shows in load profiles |
| D96 | The engine splash (ZN-446) | **zinc.json `splash` (or `targets.<id>.splash`) becomes an uncompressed `.splash` entry of `boot.zpk`: a ZSP1 header (background, fit, minMs, fadeMs) and the image as ZIM1 rgba8, an SVG drawn at 8 px per unit; it is scaled on the CPU when presented, not pre-scaled at build time; a built program shows it first thing in `main`, `zinc run` only when it runs the program itself (interpreter, .zapp, QuickJS) and hands it to a compiled executable through `ZINC_SPLASH`; every SDL main window is created hidden and shown after its first present, then presented again (Electron's show-after-ready-to-show); the browser export puts the splash in `index.html` as the canvas's CSS background with a data URL.** Scaling at present time: the desktop surface depends on density and zoom, known only at run time, and a frame of rows works with every window HAL without a new entry; pre-scaling in the panel's format stays for fixed-panel targets. One shower per run: when `zinc run` and the executable both showed it, the window closed at the exec and came back ~370 ms later. `fadeMs` is stored for ZN-447. | A fixed-panel target (fbdev, ESP32) shows a splash; ZN-447's loader screens take over the window |
| D97 | Asset hot reload in zinc dev (ZN-450) | **When a save changes only files under the assets directory of a project that packs its assets, `zinc dev` rebuilds the dev packs, compares the hash of every pack entry before and after, and sends the entries whose bytes changed (`asset <kind> <size> <name>` plus the bytes, then `end`) over a local socket pair the program inherits as `ZINC_DEV_FD` (unset and close-on-exec once read); the program swaps them in front of its packs on the libuv loop, in place for resident entries and atlas page images (same id, new pixels), and raises a host event per name for `assets.onReload`; anything else still restarts.** Bytes rather than paths, so a device link (`zinc dev --device`, TCP) can carry the same lines; entries chosen by hash rather than by a source-to-entry map, because one sprite change rewrites a whole atlas page and its table. An import error prints and keeps the old asset. | QuickJS gets host events; a project without packs wants reload of its baked loose images |
| D98 | Canvas 2D backend of the web environment, text-first tier (ZN-570) | **canvas_ity (ISC, pinned at 8be29cb, three recorded patches) draws everything; our text tier shapes and passes glyph outlines as paths.** Scores A 42, B 55, C 58, D 64, E 50 in the task notes. Measured: a 34-character line into 512 x 128 in 1.3 ms, a 640 x 480 fill in 4 to 5 ms, SSIM against headless Chrome 0.976 to 0.989 (text scene, PixiJS 8, Phaser 3, p5 2D). | The full tier (ZN-571, ZN-572) decides again after a full-screen p5 measure. |
| D99 | Where Canvas 2D lives (ZN-570) | **Linked into zinc (zn_qjs), not a `libzn_canvas2d` module as the task said:** the text tier is already in the binary and zinc grows by 94 KB (0.7 %). | Move it behind a module if the full tier grows it much. |
| D100 | The loader stage (ZN-447) | **zinc.json `loading` becomes a generated Zinc module (`zinc.json.loading.ts`, made by the CLI only when the file compiled is the project's entry) that `loadProgram` initialises before every module of the entry, as if the entry imported it first; QuickJS evaluates it first too, so the interpreter, a built executable and QuickJS run one source. It draws its screen each frame while the declared groups preload (`loading.groups`, else `boot` when declared, else every group), holds `minMs`, and cross-fades its last frame into the program's first frames through a new zinc:gfx `overlay()`; no loader when the groups are already resident or finish within the first 16 ms. Default screens ship as the `plugins/loader` plugin (`zinc:loader` bar and splash, `zinc:loader/ui`); the plugin name lives in the CLI, not the frontend (RULES §7).** Rejected: a C++ loop in the host (it cannot run Zinc screens) and deferring the main module's initialisation (IR changes). | A program that wants its own loader logic beyond a screen module; the browser export and the ESP32 core run the stage |
| D101 | Gamepads in the window host (ZN-574) | **SDL3 is built with joystick, gamepad, HIDAPI and the virtual joystick as SDL's defaults have them, haptic off** (SDL ties the virtual driver, which the tests use, to HIDAPI). Gamepads are zinc:gfx rows and `navigator.getGamepads()` with the W3C standard mapping. Cost: `libzn_window.dylib` 2,141,424 to 2,532,656 bytes (+391 KB). | A real pad was never attached: only the SDL-to-W3C table is proven. |
| D102 | Language forms beyond the prototype (ZN-160) | **Each form is lowered in the frontend onto what the IR already has: a rest parameter is an array parameter with a `rest` flag on the function type, the checker packing the trailing arguments into an array literal at the call site; a tagged template becomes a call with a `string[]` of the cooked strings (`TemplateStringsArray` is `string[]`), `String.raw` the template with its backslashes doubled; for...in a for...of over a key list taken from the static type (whole-program view) or, for Dyn values, from the prelude's `__dynKeys`; an object literal with get/set or with methods that use `this` an instance of a class generated for it; labels map onto the loops' break and continue targets.** Nothing changes in the IR, the VM or the AOT, so typed programs pay nothing (the 21 IR goldens are unchanged). Costs taken: the object literal prints as its generated class and its members carry no closure (a getter reading a local of the enclosing function is refused, Z0005); for...in lists the static type's fields. | Methods that carry a closure; a program whose for...in must see a subclass's fields through its base type |
| D103 | Full Canvas 2D backend (ZN-571) | **The full tier stays on canvas_ity, patched and completed in our layer; no Skia, no ThorVG.** Scores: Skia 50, compositor over ThorVG 52, canvas_ity kept 57. The deciding number: p5 1.11.13 with 1,000 ellipses at 800 x 600 takes 30 to 37 ms a frame on QuickJS with every context method empty, so no backend reaches 60 fps there; the script is the bound, not the rasteriser. Taken beside other builds (load 6 to 11), ratios only. Skia was not built and ThorVG not prototyped. | Revisit if the JIT engine (ZN-586) makes the rasteriser the bound, or a demo needs full-window Canvas 2D at 60 fps without WebGL: then Skia as an on-demand module. |
| D104 | Tile maps (ZN-443) | **A built-in importer `"as": "tilemap"` (yyjson) reads Tiled `.tmj`/`.tsj` (layer data as arrays or base64, plain, zlib, gzip, zstd; groups, parallax, tint, typed object properties) and LDtk `.ldtk` (all levels and worlds, IntGrid, auto tiles, entities with fields) into a text table `<map>.tilemap`; each tile a map uses becomes its own atlas frame through the sprite atlas (D95), extruded and trimmed, with a transposed copy for Tiled's diagonal flip, so drawing only mirrors; the pure-Zinc `zinc:tilemap` draws the cells in view. `.tmx`/`.tsx` (XML) are refused with the advice to save as JSON: no XML parser is vendored.** Built-in against a Zinc script importer, scored per RULES §4: 63 to 52 (no script-importer mechanism exists yet: finding the files a map names, putting tiles in the atlas); the table format would stay, so zinc:tilemap would not change. One frame per used tile against the whole tileset as one frame: no seams when scaled, only used tiles packed, no rotation at draw time. Text table as in D95. | User-defined importers or a second data format; maps over about a million cells (binary u16 layers); users asking for .tmx (tmxlite or pugixml) |
| D105 | Plugins for artists and makers: OSC, serial, MIDI (ZN-716, ZN-717, ZN-715) | **OSC is Zinc code on zinc:socket, no library, as `zinc:osc/full` beside the built-in `zinc:osc` of ZN-085; serial is POSIX termios behind one file per platform (libserialport is LGPL); MIDI is RtMidi 6.0.0 (MIT), three unmodified files in `plugins/midi/vendor`, built with exceptions.** Scores in each task's notes; the rejected serial candidates were scored from memory. | No instrument, board or adapter was attached: loopback, a pseudo-terminal and CoreMIDI virtual ports only. One `zinc:osc` instead of two is a line for later. |
| D106 | Windows toolchain (ZN-714) | **Windows programs are built with the pinned zig for x86_64-windows-gnu (mingw-w64), linked statically, not MSVC:** the same pinned compiler as every target, no Visual Studio or SDK, no redistributable. A console program links today (1.1 MB, system DLLs only); nothing has run on Windows. | Revisit if a plugin needs an MSVC-only SDK. |
| D107 | Threads in zinc:physics3d (ZN-583) | **A world steps on one thread unless it asks: `threads: n` runs Jolt's own JobSystemThreadPool with n-1 workers plus the caller, `threads: 0` one per physical core (`hw.physicalcpu` on macOS); a threaded world follows Jolt's determinism rules (listeners under a mutex, collision steps numbered by a step listener, contacts sorted by step, then ended ones, then value; moved bodies by index), so drop.out is byte for byte the same on 1, 2, 4 and 12 threads and on the three engines.** Not on by default: 20 bodies step in 0.056 ms on one thread against 0.14 to 0.15 ms on six, and the pinball relies on the one-thread contact order. One per core rather than per hardware thread: 12 threads 4.76 ms against 3.84 ms for 6 (medians of 5, loaded machine). The contact listener is set only when a body asks for events (-1.9%% instructions). | A program stepping several large worlds (one shared pool); Linux, where hardware_concurrency counts SMT threads |
| D108 | Textures (ZN-439) | **basisu 2.50 is the encoder hub, a pinned tool built from its checked source archive with the pinned zig (no SSE, OpenCL or astcenc) into `~/.zinc/toolchains`; the `texture` importer takes the target profile's `texture` list in release builds (rgba8 in dev, a rule's `format` wins), makes mipmaps with stb_image_resize2 2.18 (alpha-weighted, linear light) and premultiplies alpha by default; 16- and 32-bit layouts are made in-process, bc1/bc3/bc7/etc1/etc2/astc/pvrtc through basisu (UASTC per level, `-export_ktx`) at a fixed 4 encoder threads; ZIM1 (`include/zn/zim.h`) carries every native block format with mipmap and sRGB flags so one reader maps all of them in place, KTX2 only for the browser profile; the GL replay uploads blocks where the driver lists the format (ETC1 as ETC2 RGB8 where only ETC2 exists), else bcdec/etcdec decode on the CPU; atlas pages stay rgba8 with straight alpha (D95).** An image with alpha takes the first format of the list that has alpha, an opaque one the first or a later smaller one without alpha (`bc7|bc1`). Encoder scored 64 against 49 and 49 (report section 9); delivery scored per RULES §4: pinned sources built with zig 61, vendoring 13 MB of sources 52, linking the encoder into zinc 46. ASTC and PVRTC have no CPU decoder: the software raster shows them transparent with one warning. | The Pi runs short of CMA memory; quality complaints (astcenc, bc7enc_rdo, etcpak as options); aarch64 gives other bytes; basisu's first use (190 MB of sources, ~6 min build) becomes a problem (prebuilt through the signed index, ZN-337) |
| D109 | Models (ZN-441) | **gltfpack is a pinned tool built with the pinned zig from the meshoptimizer v1.3 archive (the vendored library's SHA-256, without Basis or WebP), sharing basisu's builder (D108); the `model` importer takes a model's images out first and sends them through the texture importer (entries `<model>.<n>.<ext>` the model points to by URI), then writes `format: "glb"` (default, for three: `gltfpack -cc -kn`, quantised attributes and EXT_meshopt_compression) or `format: "zmesh"` (for zinc:3d: `gltfpack -noq` read back with cgltf and flattened into `include/zn/zmesh.h`: float positions, normals, uvs, colours, 16-bit indices in parts of at most 65535 vertices, copied as they are by `meshLoad`); the three plugin decodes meshopt with the engine's vendored decoder, compiled in through `-I <engine>/third_party`.** Delivery scored per RULES §4: built with zig 65, vendoring its sources and shipping a second binary 56, linking it into zinc 48. Decoder in three: compiled from the engine's third_party 67, copied into plugins/three/vendor 60, no compression 47 (`zn_meshopt` is a CMake library plugins cannot link). The .zmesh keeps zinc:3d's float layout so both renderers take it as is; the int16 layout of the design waits for the software renderer. | Pi 1, PSP or 3DS need int16 positions; a program moves named parts (a node table); plugin keys must cover engine third_party files |
| D110 | Speed of JavaScript libraries (owner 2026-10-12, on `examples/js-p5` at 40 fps: the number of boxes of a sketch must not matter; "we want a fast QuickJS or our VM fast", with JIT support in QuickJS and a JavaScriptCore engine as an option; reviews D13, D36, D44, D55, D56 and section 3; reopens ZN-586) | **Two routes, both kept. (1) QuickJS stays the engine of every target and the reference, and is made fast in our tree: first a compiler of its bytecode, ahead of time (ZN-723): the operand stack and the locals as values, borrowed references, a cache per property site, direct calls, then number types behind guards; hot functions only, compiled and cached as D43 does, the interpreter staying the semantics and the fallback. The same compiler gets a machine-code backend, a JIT, afterwards, where memory may be executable; fast paths in QuickJS's runtime go in the patch queue. (2) JavaScriptCore is an optional engine behind the same host table and web environment (ZN-586): a module beside zinc, the system framework on macOS, never required. Libraries do not move to the Zinc VM. D13, D36 and D56 stand; D55's no-go covers the copy of the interpreter it measured, not a compiler.** Measured on the M1 Pro (load 4.5 to 6, one run each: to retake alone; `docs/reports/zinc-next-perf.md`). js-p5 (144 lit boxes, 800 x 500 window, 1600 x 1000 canvas, 120 Hz display): 25 ms a frame, three refreshes: script 17.2 ms (its WebGL calls 1.6), canvas image upload 1.2, swap 0.5, the rest waits for the refresh; of the script's samples inside zinc the interpreter loop is 57% and QuickJS's runtime 43% (property definition, object creation, malloc and free: p5 copies its state at every `push()`); p5 1.11 makes 6,480 `setUniform`, 3,312 `_arraysEqual`, 1,152 `Matrix.copy` and 4,301 WebGL calls a frame. 60 fps needs the script 1.3x faster, 120 fps 2.9x. The ceilings, same files (`bench/qjs`), ms per frame, QuickJS / JavaScriptCore without JIT / V8 jitless / JavaScriptCore / V8: three 4.97 / 2.34 / 3.09 / 0.204 / 0.271; sprite 8.02 / 3.74 / 8.29 / 0.250 / 0.477; objects 0.747 / 0.598 / 0.463 / 0.118 / 0.100; p5shape 8.45 / 6.05 / 4.75 / 0.31 / 1.71: the best interpreters are 1.0 to 2.1x QuickJS, the JITs 5 to 32x. The system JavaScriptCore embedded in a program runs without its JIT (6.16 ms on p5shape) unless the executable carries `com.apple.security.cs.allow-jit` (0.41 ms; an ad hoc signature is enough). Read the same day. PocketJS (commit 8a08f203, 2026-10-10) has no fast QuickJS: an interpreter with no JIT and no bytecode, kept out of the frame (cores in Rust own state and time, one crossing per steady frame, styles and keyframes baked), and its new MicroTS compiles a typed TypeScript subset to Rust with no guest engine, which is Zinc's typed AOT; it runs no browser library. Longbridge's quickjs-jit 0.12.11 (MIT; 59,000 lines of Rust on Cranelift and a 2,800-line patch of quickjs-ng; +3.7 MB; test262 identical) is a JIT inside QuickJS: 32 to 45x on numeric kernels and 0.99 to 1.00x on its own realistic application panel, because its policy refuses 124 of about 255 opcodes (closure variables, `this`, `typeof`, `for of`, `try`) and every call returns through `JS_CallInternal` (its analysis of 2026-09-27). Options scored (RULES section 4: fit x3, performance x3, size x2, maintainability x2, licence and supply x2, portability x1, effort x1); (a) and (c) are both taken, they do not exclude each other: (c) JavaScriptCore as an optional engine: 4,5,4,3,4,2,3 = 54; (a) a compiler of QuickJS bytecode, ahead of time, a JIT backend after it: 5,3,4,2,5,5,1 = 52; (e) QuickJS as it is, with accelerators per library: 1,1,5,5,5,5,5 = 46; (b) libraries on the Zinc VM: 3,2,3,2,5,5,1 = 41; (d) a JIT inside QuickJS before the compiler: 3,3,4,1,5,2,1 = 41; (f) adopting Longbridge's JIT now: 3,2,3,2,5,3,3 = 41. | D44's levers are spent: its boundary fixes are done and the binding is 9% of js-p5's script, its spike gave 1.08 to 1.40x (D55), its caches 0.97 to 1.06x (D56), and both its revisit conditions hold; the option it kept in reserve, a JIT engine on desktop, is the only measured way to browser speed (6 to 32x) and the owner asks for it as an option. It stays an option, QuickJS the default: iOS and consoles forbid a JIT (without it JavaScriptCore is 1.2 to 2.1x), Windows and Linux have no system engine, macOS wants an entitlement, and one reference engine keeps the tests deterministic. For QuickJS itself no interpreter gets there, so the lever is native code, and D55 named what the hand translations did that a copy does not (L1 5.9x, L1.5 7.4x, speculative 85x on the sprite loop; 4.9x on three's `multiplyMatrices`): compiler work, which D55's revisit condition (2x or more on library frames on a target without a JIT) now asks for. Ahead of time first, as D36 and D43 chose for typed code: every target runs it, and the profiler dump (ZN-598), the bind hook (patch 0002) and the test262 gate exist. The JIT is a backend of that compiler: Longbridge's shows that emitting machine code is not the hard part, covering closures, `this`, calls and objects is, and that work is the same ahead of time and at run time. Not the Zinc VM: its frontend stops at line 3885 of three.core.js and its `Dyn` objects are hash maps without caches, slower than QuickJS on property code (dynsum 39.2 against 30.2 ms); unmodified libraries need the whole of JavaScript's object model and library, a second JavaScript engine; the VM stays the engine of typed code, where D13 and D36 hold. What route (1) does not promise: expected 2 to 3x on library frames at L1.5 (the report's estimate, not measured), allocation-heavy code stays bound by QuickJS's runtime, and browser speed needs speculation and a moving collector: on desktop that is route (2). | ZN-723's gate fails (below 2x on the three and matter-js frames with values, borrowing and caches): then JavaScriptCore carries desktop speed and the compiler is rescored; ZN-586's spike shows the JavaScriptCore binding costs what the JIT gains (4,301 WebGL calls a frame on js-p5), or Apple restricts the entitlement; Longbridge's JIT covers closures, `this` and calls and gains on library frames (then score adopting it against our backend); quickjs-ng gains a register bytecode or caches. |
| D111 | The GL replay on GLES 2 (ZN-725, owner 2026-10-12 with a Pi 3B+ plugged in) | **On GLES 2 every mode of the replay is drawn by a program of its own, built at first use from that mode's block of the shader source, and the antialiased edges of POLY and STROKE are thin strips whose coverage comes from the vertices. Desktop GL keeps its single program and its pixels. display-gl's default renderer stays `cpu` on GLES 2 and display-fbdev's bands stay the default display of a Pi 3.** | Measured on the Pi 3B+ at 1400 MHz (hero, present p50): navigation driving 181 to 25 ms, home 31 to 15, gallery 33 to 23, first frame 1.1 to 0.29 s. Options measured: program classes (UI core and vector) were slower than one program per mode, since a GPU without branches still pays a class's ladder; 4x MSAA with plain triangles 35.5 ms against 29.7 for strips on navigation, and it only covers frames drawn straight into the window. The software bands still lead (19.1 ms) at the price of 289% of a core against 56% and of heat, and the GL renderer loses on gallery (the rounded clip reads the framebuffer) and has had no long run on partially changing screens: hence the defaults. Numbers and the open points: docs/reports/raspberry-pi.md, backlog/later.md. |
| D112 | Text under a scaling matrix (ZN-726, owner 2026-10-12 on museum-table; amends D71) | **A TEXT drawn through a matrix that scales takes its glyphs from the first size, not below its size on screen, of a ladder a quarter of an octave apart (8, 10, 11, 13, 16, 19, 23, 27, 32, 38, 45, 54, 64, 76, 91, 108, 128 px), drawn at 84 to 103% of their size, with the advances of the base font times the scale: a text keeps its line breaks at every zoom and is sharp. Within 0.84 to 1.03 and under a matrix that only turns, the text's own glyphs serve, byte for byte as before. At most 64 fonts are made for scaled text (`ZRT_SCALED_FONTS`, 0 on small targets), never freed.** | Scored as RULES 4 says: the font's own bitmaps resampled (D71) 55, blurred, the owner's complaint; a font per exact size 46, every visible glyph rasterized at each frame of a zoom; the ladder with the nearest size 55 (63% of the edge energy of the text set directly at x3); the ladder with the first size not below 58 (77 to 83%), chosen; an SDF atlas 46 (a dependency and a path in both rasterizers); outlines as polygons 46 (cost per frame). zinc:ui drew a scaled Text in the nearest font the program had and never through the matrix: it does now. Revisit if a zoom at rest must equal text set directly, or if a Pi hitches at the ladder steps (backlog/later.md). |
| D113 | Boxes under a matrix that only scales and moves (ZN-730, owner 2026-10-12 on museum-table's film; amends D71) | **Under a matrix without rotation or skew (b = c = 0, a and d positive) a RECT, BORDER, SHADOW or IMAGE is recorded at its place and size on the screen, as the command given directly would be, and not as an XFORM. A radius, a border's width and a shadow's blur need a uniform scale (a = d); TEXT keeps D112; a gradient paint record keeps the matrix.** | The XFORM path draws pixel by pixel through the inverse matrix: on a Pi 3B+ the film's picture (854x480 drawn 500x281) cost 82 ms of CPU under a scale-only matrix against 13.5 drawn directly, and every card of museum-table is drawn through one matrix since D112. Rejected: a fast case inside `draw_xform` (the GL replay and the damage would still see a bounding box and a payload). Seen and accepted: an unturned image that moves or zooms lands on whole pixels, as every direct image does (three museum frames and one image_rect golden changed, by 1 to 2 levels inside the picture and on its edge rows). Revisit if edges that snap show during a zoom. The other half of the film's 11 pictures a second was not the raster: swscale read the V4L2 decoder's capture buffer, mapped without a cache, at 85 ms a picture; zinc:video copies the frame into ordinary memory first (10 ms). |
| D114 | Zero-copy video: a display's hardware layers (ZN-731, owner 2026-10-12: "zero-copy, no millisecond lost without a reason") | **A display driver may offer hardware layers (`HalDisplay.layers`, runtime/include/hal.h). zinc:video then asks the decoder for its pictures as dmabufs (DRM PRIME from `h264_v4l2m2m`) and gives the runtime image a `HalPicture` instead of pixels; the runtime offers each upright, whole IMAGE of such a picture to the driver before the frame is drawn; a driver that takes it shows the dmabuf on a plane and the rasterizer writes a hole there. On a Raspberry Pi the layers are overlay planes of the Hardware Video Scaler inside display-fbdev: the picture on one, the software frame on an ARGB plane above it, taken only while a hardware picture is drawn, with plain KMS ioctls; the mode and the console's primary plane are never touched. What is drawn over the film stays in front: the rows it touches are drawn a second time over white holes and the two renders give each pixel's alpha. A film turned, blended, with rounded corners of its own or drawn in part, and any display without layers, take the path of D113.** | Scored as RULES 4 says (fit, performance, size, maintainability, licence, portability, effort): planes inside display-fbdev 64 (5,5,5,4,5,3,3), chosen: programs and their zinc.json do not change, a screen without a film is today's fbdev byte for byte, a killed program leaves the console (the kernel removes a closed client's planes: checked with SIGKILL); a new display-kms driver on libdrm that owns the mode 53 (a second driver with its own input, bands and statistics, which must restore the console itself); the decoder or the ISP giving the picture at the drawn size over /dev/fb0 48 (a copy out of the uncached buffer and the raster stay); an EGLImage texture in display-gl 45 (on VideoCore IV the UI is fastest in software, D111). Rejected too: an alpha channel in the rasterizer, a "nothing translucent over the film" rule (museum-table's timeline is translucent: its film would never have left the CPU), libdrm. Measured on the Pi 3B+: museum-table's film 38.3 to 3.45 ms of CPU a picture, no pixel of it read by the CPU. Revisit when a film must stay off the CPU while turned, when a second driver needs layers, or when a compositor is the display. |
| D116 | The device agent: pairing, sessions, discovery (ZN-729.01, owner 2026-10-12: a daemon found "in a secure way: Bonjour, an encrypted protocol, a code", an adb over the network) | **Pairing is TLS 1.2 with EC J-PAKE (the 6-digit code as its password), sessions are TLS 1.3 with a pre-shared key and an ephemeral ECDHE exchange, both from the mbedTLS already vendored (`MBEDTLS_KEY_EXCHANGE_ECJPAKE_ENABLED` turned on in its config); the pairing gives each workstation an identity and a 32-byte key of its own. Discovery is DNS-SD over mDNS (`_zinc._tcp`) with mjansson/mdns 1.4.3 (public domain, one header) vendored in `third_party/mdns`. One TCP connection per operation, nothing multiplexed.** Why a PAKE: a short code used any other way (a MAC over the channel binding, a fingerprint to compare) is broken off line by whoever sits between the two machines in the time of one handshake; with J-PAKE a wrong code is one failed guess, and the agent allows three per code.
