It is the design of adb's wireless pairing (SPAKE2, then TLS) and of HomeKit and Matter (a PAKE once, long-term keys after). Why keys and not certificates: both ends must prove themselves, a 32-byte secret per pair does it with `mbedtls_ssl_conf_psk` alone (no X.509 to write, no key pair to store), and forgetting a workstation is deleting its line. Scores (fit x3, performance x3, size x2, maintainability x2, licence x2, portability x1, effort x1, 1-5). Pairing and sessions: mbedTLS J-PAKE then PSK 5,5,5,4,5,5,4 = **67**; Noise with a PAKE written over monocypher 4,5,5,2,5,5,2 = 58 (a protocol of our own: RULES 3 says no); mutual TLS with self-signed certificates and a fingerprint compared by eye 2,5,5,3,5,5,3 = 55 (a device without a screen cannot confirm, a short fingerprint is forged); OpenSSH on the device and libssh2 in the client 2,4,3,3,4,3,3 = 44 (keys and a server to set up by hand: what `tools/device` needs today). Discovery: mjansson/mdns 5,5,5,4,5,5,4 = **67**; the ZINC1 UDP beacon of display-remote 3,5,5,4,5,5,5 = 62 (no standard tool sees it; the owner asked for Bonjour); the system libraries (dns_sd.h, Avahi) 4,5,4,3,4,2,2 = 53 (Avahi is missing on many boards and from a zig cross build). Measured on the loopback interface (M1 Pro, 2026-10-12): a session with one request 35 ms, a shell command 43 ms; the agent and its client are 227 KB of code in `zinc`. mbedTLS has no thread support in our build, so the agent's threads share one lock around its calls. `tls::read` now goes on after a TLS 1.3 session ticket instead of returning with the bytes behind it unread (found here; it concerned every TLS 1.3 client of host/tls.cpp). Details: `docs/reports/zinc-next-agent.md`. | Revisit when sessions need to encrypt in parallel (build mbedTLS with `MBEDTLS_THREADING_PTHREAD`), when a device needs IPv6, or if mbedTLS drops EC J-PAKE (then SPAKE2+ through PSA `psa_pake`). |
| D115 | The device agent: updates, the dev loop, the inspector, Atelier (ZN-729.03 to .06; decided by the agents that worked them, recorded at integration) | **Updates: an app whose zinc.json has `update.publicKey` is replaced only by a `.zapp` that verifies with the installed app's key and has a higher version: pushed, with the detached signature of `zinc sign` sent in the install request; from its channel, with the signed manifest of `zinc publish`. The first install needs no signature. The version replaced stays as `previous.zapp` (a hard link) and comes back when the update ends with an error within 5 s of its first run (`ZINC_AGENT_TRIAL_MS`).** A paired workstation already has the user's rights, so the signature guards an app against a wrong or stale build, not against its owner; no cryptography was written (`zn::tc::verifyBytes`, `fetchManifest`, `downloadUpdate`). The service is per user, never root: a launchd agent at login, a systemd user unit with lingering, `KillMode=process` so apps outlive the agent. **Dev loop: `zinc dev --device` keeps the loop of `zinc dev` on the workstation and makes `zinc pack` the check; each save sends the members whose SHA-256 changed and the agent restarts the program. The agent refuses a workstation with another engine version (the bytecode is compiled there). The dev program is not an app: no pid file, it ends with its session.** **Inspector: the agent picks a free port, gives it to every app it starts as `ZINC_DEVTOOLS_PORT` and keeps it as the second line of the pid file** (the log cannot say it: it is cut back past 4 MiB). `shot` speaks the DevTools protocol through a few lines of Zinc run by the engine and `monitor` pipes the followed log into `zinc monitor`: no WebSocket or telemetry code was added in C++. `top` is a stream of `info`. **Atelier (ZN-729.06) drives the CLI through zinc:process and holds no protocol code: Run on a device is `zinc device <name> deploy`, its follower ending at the log's `[exited n]` line; Debug is Run plus `zinc device inspect`, not `zinc dev --device` (a dev session is not an app, so there is nothing to forward).** Measured on the loopback interface (M1 Pro, load average 10, other trees building): a saved source runs on the device 211 to 250 ms after the save. Details: `docs/reports/zinc-next-agent.md`. | Revisit the trial of 5 s after a measure on a Pi 3 with a large `.zapp`; the unsigned first install if `--no-shell` devices must refuse an owner's own replacement; the pack's target when a workstation drives a device of another architecture. |
| D117 | Frames that tear in bands on the Pi's screen (ZN-735, owner 2026-10-12: fast animations show the picture cut in bands) | **On a display with KMS, display-fbdev writes every frame into the buffer that is not shown and one atomic commit shows it at the refresh, with or without a video picture; `ZINC_FBDEV_FLIP=0` gives back the write into `/dev/fb0`.** The cause: a frame was converted straight into the buffer on the screen, 16 rows a band on four threads, and the wait meant to time that write to the vertical blank returns at once on the DSI screen of a Pi (the kernel's framebuffer emulation waits for another CRTC, ZN-731), so the scan-out read bands of the new frame beside bands of the old. The cure was already in the driver for frames with a video picture (D114, zero-copy video); it is now the path of every frame, scaled surfaces included. Measured on the Pi 3B+ at 1400 MHz, hero's navigation, one binary, two pairs: 39.9 and 40.0 frames a second into `/dev/fb0`, 38.5 and 37.9 flipped (3.5 to 5% less: the display's buffers are 32 bits a pixel where `/dev/fb0` is 16, convert 1.44 to 2.07 ms); hero's tour 59.3 to 59.8, pocket-hero 59.7 to 60.0. A partial update stays partial (pocket-hero: 1.3% of the surface damaged, 2.5% written). A correct picture at 4% fewer frames was taken over a torn one. Proven by the driver's own count (260 commits, 0 frames into the buffer on the screen); that the bands are gone is for the owner's eyes: the task is in Review. | Revisit with RGB565 buffers while no picture is shown (the 4%), a third buffer so a frame's conversion does not wait for the refresh before it, and a display without a free plane (it still tears: panning `/dev/fb0`). |

| D118 | Leap Motion as a Zinc plugin (ZN-738, owner 2026-10-13) | **Load the installed LeapC SDK dynamically behind the ordinary native-module ABI; copy the latest hand snapshot with nonblocking polling, and put projection/gestures in the Zinc app.** Direct optional SDK: scores (fit, performance, size, maintainability, licence, portability, effort) 5,5,5,4,4,3,5 = **64**; directly linked SDK 5,5,4,4,4,3,4 = 61 (a missing installation prevents app startup); a subprocess/socket bridge 4,3,4,3,4,4,2 = 49 (extra process and protocol). The supplied build/leap_clion is an example executable; its CMake points at the official installed SDK. No proprietary headers or binaries are copied into Zinc. Simulation is explicit; missing/stale tracking clears hands. Event lifetime follows [Ultraleap LeapC](https://docs.ultraleap.com/api-reference/tracking-api/leapc-guide/using-leapc.html). | Revisit for another tracking backend or the cross-platform pass. |
| D119 | The GPU layer for low-end and console GPUs (owner, 2026-10-16; ZN-741, ZN-627) | **Keep our own renderer: the command lists replayed by one back end per GPU API, the GLES 2 replay as the base for every GLES 2 device (Pi 3, HP TouchPad, Nexus 7 2012), choices made by a capability record and a per-GPU-family tuning table, never by platform name; contexts come from one provider per platform (SDL3 on desktop, Android and iOS; KMS/EGL; webOS SDL 1.2). The layer is written so it can leave the engine as a project of its own: its own directory and CMake target, a C ABI, no dependency on the runtime beyond the command-list header, its own tests on fake GL strings.** Not bgfx (its shader tool chain and size, scored in `docs/reports/parity/04-library-choices.md` §2.10), not sokol_gfx (no GLES 2), not ANGLE (size), not SDL_GPU (no GL). Why separable: the owner plans back ends for connected platforms that no library covers together, CHIP (Mali-400, GLES 2), PS Vita (GXM), 3DS (PICA200 via citro3d), Wii (GX), Wii U (GX2), Switch (deko3d or NVN); PocketJS draws its 10-op list with one small back end per such GPU (`docs/reports/hardware/pocketjs-pocket3d.md`), the model followed here. Design: `docs/reports/zinc-next-mobile-core.md` §4.1, with the Tegra 3 measures (no fragment highp, depth 16, no packed depth-stencil, 2048 textures). | Revisit when a second engine wants the layer (then extract it), or when one back end per API costs more than a shared library would (count the lines per back end). |
| D120 | The engine as a library for app shells (ZN-620, coordinator 2026-10-16) | **One C ABI in SDL3's main-callbacks shape for every shell: `zn_app_init` runs the top level up to the loop, `zn_app_iterate` is the vsync tick (one frame of `__gfxStep` or one turn of `__loopStep`), `zn_app_event` queues SDL events for the window HAL, `zn_app_quit` ends the run; `zn_app_main` = init then iterate until done, so desktop behaviour and goldens are unchanged.** iOS owns its run loop and SDL3 recommends the callbacks there and on the web; Android works either way, so one form for all (Flutter's embedder model; `docs/reports/zinc-next-mobile-core.md` §3). App mode reaches the prelude as bit 1 of `host.loopReal` (no new runtime row); the step is found by name (`kAppStepFunction`, a root of the dead-function pass). The library (`zn_app`) has its own copies of rt and of the host built with `ZN_APP_LIBRARY` rather than reusing `zn_rt` (it pulls zbc, ir and the frontend through CMake) and `zn_host_gfx` (process spawning, dlopen of macOS frameworks); `nm` finds no dlopen, fork or exec (`tests/t0/app_library.sh`). AOT programs only for now. | Revisit when a second consumer needs rt without the compiler (move zbc.cpp into its own archive), or when the player (interpreter + `.zapp`) lands. |
| D121 | The heap of a phone app (ZN-622, 2026-10-16) | **The Android shell sets `ZINC_HEAP` to the profile's heap (128 MiB for `android`, baked by the export as `ZN_APP_HEAP_MB`), capped at a quarter of `SDL_GetSystemRAM()`; a `ZINC_HEAP` given at launch overrides it; the same 128 MiB is the program's mimalloc budget.** The 512 MiB desktop region cannot be reserved on a 1 GB 32-bit device (Nexus 7 2012). Left: mimalloc prints an "unable to allocate aligned OS memory" warning for the budget at every start (later.md). | Revisit when a program declares more, or on iOS (jetsam limits). |
| D122 | Hot compile mode for Fast Refresh (ZN-742.01) | **One switch, `ZINC_HOT=1`; outside it the ZBC is byte-identical. The loader handles `import.meta.hot`: outside hot mode the statements that need it become empty nodes (prelude scans do not see them); in hot mode it becomes zinc:hot's `hotModule(path)` through an implicit import, so the hooks stay real module functions with keys. Each module gets an `@init:<path>` function and main runs the prelude, the inits, then `__runLoop()`; every top-level variable is a global so module state outlives its init. Keys: `<path>::<name>`, `<path>::thunk:<name>`, `<path>#<offset>` for a lambda (plus ` in <generic instance>` and `.n` for async desugaring), `::<name>` for structural classes.** A prelude instead of an import would hide the hooks from the key scheme; without the suffixes keys collided on the desktop-app. | Revisit if a duplicate key shows up in a real app; ZN-742.02 refuses a swap on a shared key. |
| D123 | Fast Refresh swap (ZN-742.02) | **The `zinc dev` child recompiles in process inside the frame-end hook; the swap waits for `pauseLoop` (the loop ends, the window stays) and runs on an empty stack in the same Machine. Live objects are mapped to the new classes by key, field names and types; closures of changed files become zombie classes whose methods trap and which must be dead once the inits ran. A change to a library module or the prelude, a new native export, different baked resources or an undisposed top-level effect restarts with a reason; `render`, `renderWindow`, `onFrame` and `import.meta.hot` calls are allowed at top level.** Memory-safe without stable function indices: every refusal is a restart, never a wrong mapping. Measured: 114-126 ms (two-file gfx), 330-400 ms (Solid), 180-300 ms (game-2d), against ~570 ms for a restart of the desktop-app. | Revisit if the compile pause in the frame hook is felt (compile on a thread) or if zombies refuse common edits. |
| D122 | One program dims and keeps the screen on, on webOS and Android (owner 2026-10-16, examples/music on the Nexus 7) | **The screen kept on and the backlight are device queries of zinc:sys (`wakelock`, `backlight`), answered by a simulated device first, then the webOS display driver (zinc-helper), then the host's own answers (Android: SDL's screen saver for wake lock, the window's `screenBrightness` set on the activity's UI thread, 0 dark, -1 the system's). zinc:webos `setBacklight` and `navigator.wakeLock` both go through them, so a webOS program runs on Android with no change to its code; `setPerformance` and `openPort` answer false off webOS (nothing to raise or open on a phone).** Chosen over an `android` target in zinc:webos and zinc:device: no new plugin dependency on the TouchPad build, one answer per query in the host. Plugins that only use the network require `net`, not `process` (socket, osc, sonos, spotify). HTTPS on Android reads the system's root certificates (`/system/etc/security/cacerts`, plus the user's added ones). | Revisit when a third platform needs another service of zinc:webos (then a common module `zinc:screen`), or when a program needs steady clocks (`setSustainedPerformanceMode`). |
| D124 | Fast Refresh components and boundaries (ZN-742.03) | **In hot mode only, calls to the project's components get a `_hotC` proxy: when the file that declares one runs again, its instances are disposed and rendered again under the same owner, so parents and siblings keep their state (solid-refresh's rule). A module that exports only code and types (functions, components, classes, interfaces, type aliases) is a boundary: importers reach it by symbol and live objects move to the new classes by key (D123), so only exported values make importers run again.** This departs from React Native, which re-runs the importers of any module exporting more than components (owner report: editing game-2d's `scenes.ts` reset the round). The desktop-app's `state.ts`, `router.ts` and `main.tsx` still restart on their top-level effects: re-running `state.ts` would reset the state a refresh keeps, an allow-list cannot tell a call that replaces (`setColorMode`) from one that stacks (menus, listeners), an empty `dispose` in the template would teach users to silence the check, and `main.tsx` restarting matches RN's `index.js`. | Revisit if users edit state modules often. |
| D123 | When an Android app keeps the screen on; kiosk and fullscreen (owner 2026-10-16, examples/music on the Nexus 7) | **An Android app lets the screen sleep as a phone app or a web page does; a program keeps it on with `navigator.wakeLock` (zinc:sys "wakelock", D122); zinc.json `targets.android.kiosk` keeps it on, traps the back button and goes immersive full screen, `targets.android.fullscreen` only the last. Both are baked by `zinc export` as `ZN_APP_ENV` for every build, debug or release.** SDL keeps the screen on for every app by default (FLAG_KEEP_SCREEN_ON unless `SDL_HINT_VIDEO_ALLOW_SCREENSAVER`), measured on the Nexus 7 (`fl=#1810980`), so the shell sets the hint unless the app is a kiosk. Immersive sticky comes from SDLActivity behind `SDL_SetWindowFullscreen` (the HAL's existing ZINC_KIOSK path); `vsysui=0x1706`, 1280x800 with no bars. A kiosk app's own dimming (examples/music: black and window brightness 0, the next touch wakes) is unchanged. Why: Zinc's apps are apps, not games; a screen that never sleeps drains a battery on modest devices, and the program that needs it on says so. | Revisit if a game with a gamepad or a video player is seen sleeping; Home and Recents still leave a kiosk (Android's lock task mode would need a device-owner or pinning flow). |
| D124 | Runtime shaders in zinc:gfx (ZN-746, owner 2026-10-16: the lava lamp face of examples/music) | **A program's shader is a GLSL ES 1.00 fragment `main()` behind a fixed head (`v_uv`, `u_size`, `u_p[8]`), made with `shaderProgram(src, scale)` and drawn with `drawShader` as a SHADER command of the command list; the GL replay draws it (desktop GL core, display-gl, and on Android a GPU layer under the software-rastered frame, without waiting for ZN-741); without a GPU the program's own Zinc code draws the same picture at a lower resolution with `ImageFlag.Smooth`, chosen with `shaderAvailable()`. Changing only a shader's uniforms damages nothing, so the CPU rests while it animates.** Scored (RULES §4: fit x3, performance x3, size x2, maintainability x2, licence x2, portability x1, effort x1): GLSL ES 1.00 5,5,5,4,5,3,5 = **74**; an SkSL-like language of our own translated to GLSL, Metal and a CPU interpreter 4,4,2,2,5,5,1 = 54; a Zinc callback per pixel on the CPU only 2,1,5,4,5,5,4 = 52. GLSL ES 1.00 is what WebGL 1 and every GLES 2 GPU compile natively, glslang validates it, mediump-safe by construction (D119); a per-pixel GLSL interpreter is ruled out (the 80x60 field plus a smooth stretch is already 22 fps on the Nexus 7's CPU). zinc:gl (WebGL) and three.js keep their own contexts: SHADER is a 2D fill, not a GL API. Lava face on the Nexus 7: 4.0 fps before, 21.9 with raw pixels and one smooth draw, 61 fps on the GPU layer (program scale 4); `tests/t1/shader_gl.sh` compares the GPU frame with the CPU path (0% of pixels more than 24 apart). | Revisit for a Metal replay on iOS (SPIR-V from glslang through SPIRV-Cross), or for programs that need several passes. |
| D125 | The iOS project (ZN-623, ZN-624, 2026-10-16) | **`zinc export --target ios` writes a CMake project that CMake's Xcode generator turns into the Xcode project: the engine through `add_subdirectory` (zn_app with SDL3's UIKit backend), the AOT C++ of `--profile ios`, the plugins compiled from their sources, Info.plist from zinc.json, an asset catalog with the icon, `assets/` in the bundle; minimum iOS 16.3.** Scored (RULES §4): CMake with the Xcode generator 64; SDL's Xcode project plus an app project 49; a .pbxproj written by zinc 49. SDL documents iOS builds through CMake (README-cmake, `-DCMAKE_SYSTEM_NAME=iOS`), the Android export already builds the engine through CMake (one engine build description for both phones), a .pbxproj would list every engine source, and SDL's Xcode project is not vendored. Minimum iOS 16.3: the system C++ library has `std::to_chars` for doubles from 16.3 (iPhone 8 and later). Cost: the first configure takes about 5.5 minutes on a loaded machine, a build after it 41 s. hello's output equals `zinc run` (29 lines, os_log drained at quit); rn-showcase at 60 fps on the iPhone 17 simulator (software raster through SDL_Renderer), start 539 ms; 9.0 MB Debug, 8.2 MB Release. | Revisit when the configure is too slow (a prebuilt `libzn_app.a`), or for iOS 15 devices (a vendored shortest-double formatter). |
| D126 | Scrolling through the GLES 2 replay on a slow GPU (ZN-741, ZN-744, 2026-10-16, Nexus 7) | **A vertical scroll keeps the scroll area's rows rotated in the frame texture and replays only the rectangles `diff_move` names; on by default for GLES 2, off on the desktop (`ZINC_GL_MOVE=1`). One capability record and a tuning table by GPU family (`runtime/gl_caps.h`, `ZINC_GL_INFO`) choose the lean programs (Tegra 3, Adreno 2xx) and skip the window clear on non-tiling GPUs; shaders place positions relative to each primitive (mediump-safe). Android keeps the software raster by default until a replay pass costs under 1 ms.** Scored (RULES §4): rotated rows 66, moving pixels through a scratch texture 63, a tiled layer of the whole content 51, software raster with a GPU compositor 47. Why: on Tegra 3 the GPU replays slower per pixel than four A9 cores rasterise (a 1280x50 strip: 4.5 ms of GPU against ~1 ms of CPU; each pass costs 1-3 ms of CPU before any pixel) and texture uploads run at ~100 MB/s, so GLES 2 wins only by drawing less. Measured (rn-showcase, ten flings, 720 frames): GLES 2 p99 56.8 ms before, 31.6-32.5 ms after (221-285 late); the software raster p99 26.8-29.4 ms (222-263 late); at rest and in tab switches both are level (the screens' own effects and layout, 30-118 ms, dominate a switch). Tried and dropped: one depth-masked pass over the union of rectangles (GPU 7-19 ms), rotating vertex buffers (no change), a GPU compositor over the software raster (`glTexSubImage2D` 26-30 ms a frame), EGL preserved swap (refused by Tegra 3). | Revisit on GPUs whose replay beats their CPU (Mali, Adreno 3xx and later), and when a replay pass costs under 1 ms. |
| D127 | Plugins on phones (ZN-628, 2026-10-17) | **A plugin declares what a phone needs in plugin.json `android` and `ios` entries (sources: .java/.kt to Gradle, .m/.mm/.swift to Xcode; gradle dependencies; manifest permissions and fragments; minSdk; Info.plist fragments; entitlements; lifecycle; class), merged by `zinc export` (Expo's config plugins, without code); `zinc native-gen --android/--ios` writes the Java interface + JNI bridge or the ObjC/Swift protocols + ObjC++ bridge from the plugin's spec.ts (i32, u32, f64, boolean, string, void callbacks, run on the program's thread). zinc:system keeps one op table with the C++ permission gate and the simulator, plus a thin live backend per OS (Java over JNI, ObjC).** The model scored 65 in docs/reports/native-fidelity-and-plugins.md §4.4 (against 56 and 56). One op table rather than moving the module to Java keeps the permission gate and the simulator as one code. Proved: zinc:sqlite on the iOS simulator and the Android emulator (database kept across starts), a Swift and a Kotlin fixture with callbacks from another thread and lifecycle, the share sheet, opener, local notifications with the permission prompt, document and photo pickers, deep links warm and cold (Android). A plugin without an entry for the target is refused at export with its name; the Android manifest lists exactly the declared permissions. App plugin libraries are named `zn_app_plugin_<name>`; `app.urlSchemes` becomes intent filters and CFBundleURLTypes. Fixed on the way: the opener's scope check runs before the live backend (macOS opened URLs outside `scopes.opener.allow`). | Revisit if a plugin needs imperative project edits, or Promises, arrays or records across the glue. |
| D128 | Android CPU cores, steady clocks and the window's byte order (ZN-750, owner 2026-10-17, Nexus 7) | **The engine asks `Window.setSustainedPerformanceMode` while frames move (from the first damaged frame to one second after the last) and while a program asks for full speed (zinc:webos `setPerformance`, a zinc:sys `performance` query), where `PowerManager.isSustainedPerformanceModeSupported` says the device has it; nothing else: no thread spinning, no hotplug writes (an app cannot, and keeping the raster's workers runnable between frames lost frames: 365-427 late of 720 against 314-366). The development Nexus 7 keeps its four cores online through `nexus7-tune.sh` (Tegra's cpuquiet `userspace` governor; autosmp is built in but off). The window buffer is RGBX_8888 with a per-stripe red/blue swap from API 26, BGRA_8888 drawn in place below.** Cores, rn-showcase ten flings (late of 720): 1 core 474, 2 cores 443, 4 cores 240-297, the ROM's balanced 303-366; music carousel swipes (late of 390): 327, 203, 184-189, 175-199; with the tune applied 255-302. `isSustainedPerformanceModeSupported` is false on the Nexus 7 and on the API 37 emulator. Byte order: the API 37 emulator accepts HAL format 5, reports it back and composes it as RGBA (no query tells such a device apart); RGBX everywhere would cost the Nexus 7 5 ms of raster per scrolled frame (p50 13.2 against 8.3 ms). | Revisit with an API 26-36 device measured honouring BGRA (move the line), a raster that writes RGBA itself (no swap), or a device that supports the sustained mode. |
| D129 | Phone packages from zinc export (ZN-629, 2026-10-17) | **`--aab`, `--apk` and `--ipa [--simulator]` run the platform's own builders (Gradle, xcodebuild) in the exported project and check the result; `--dry-run` prints the commands in tools/sign-macos's form. Signing secrets never enter the project: the Android upload key's path and alias come from the environment or zinc.json `signing.android`, its passwords only from the environment, read by Gradle at build time (zinc.json refuses a password); without a key the debug keystore signs, and the export says Play will refuse it. iOS: ExportOptions.plist from zinc.json `signing.ios` (team, Xcode 15.3's method names with the old ones mapped, automatic signing or a named profile). The 16 KB check reads the ELF program headers of every packaged 64-bit library itself (plus `zipalign -c -P 16` for an APK) rather than trusting the link flags. PrivacyInfo.xcprivacy comes from a table of the engine's required-reason APIs verified with `nm -u` (file timestamps C617.1, system boot time 35F9.1, disk space E174.1) plus the plugins' `targets.ios.privacy`.** Scored (RULES §4): the export runs the builders and checks 63; print the commands only 50; a fastlane lane 41. Proved: an APK and an AAB (bundletool install-apks, release, non-debuggable) on the API 37 emulator, a simulator build on iOS 26.3, each printing what `zinc run` prints; the dry-runs tested with every Apple and Android tool as a trap. Uploads and a device IPA need the owner's paid accounts (commands in the ZN-629 notes). | Revisit if Play or Apple add a check zinc can run locally, or for `zinc publish --play / --app-store`. |
| D130 | Platform looks for the kits (ZN-748, owner 2026-10-16) | **Four token tables (iOS, Android Material 3, macOS, neutral) in `lib/std/platform/tokens.ts`, module `zinc:ui/platform`, read by the existing components: zinc:ui/kit's Button, Switch, Slider, Tabs and typography take the platform's sizes and shapes, and its colours only while the app keeps the kit's built-in theme; the nuxt kit changes only its Switch shape; zinc:react-native's Switch, Button and ActivityIndicator draw as each platform does. `auto` (the default) gives iOS, Android, macOS on a Mac, neutral elsewhere; zinc.json `ui.platformLook`, `ZINC_PLATFORM_LOOK`, `setPlatformLook()` or `<ThemeProvider look>` force one. Behaviour follows `ui.platformStyle()` and is not themeable: long press opens the kit ContextMenu on a touch screen, haptics where the OS's own controls give one (iOS switch, segment, long press; Android long press). On a Mac the tint is the user's accent colour (`device('accent')`).** Scored (RULES §4): token tables read by the existing components 65, colours only 61, two component sets as Flutter's Material and Cupertino 51. Android means Material 3, not React Native's older switch. The tables build their text classes at run time (the font baker bakes every literal size of the sources, library files included). Fonts apply only when the app ships them: SF Pro and Android 12+ Roboto are variable fonts the rasterizer cannot draw yet. A deterministic run on a Mac without ZINC_PLATFORM takes neutral, so no existing golden changed; `tests/t1/platform_look.sh` holds one golden per profile. | Revisit with variable fonts in runtime/ttf.cpp (then the OS's own UI font), Android 12+ dynamic colour, or a control the tables do not cover. |

| D140 | Zinc Go, a player for phones (ZN-631, owner 2026-10-16: an Expo Go for Zinc) | **Programs for the player are compiled on the developer's machine and sent as hot units (ZBC, stable keys, module graph, baked resources, zstd) over TCP by `zinc dev --lan`, Expo's model, rather than shipping the compiler and lib/std on the phone or a process per program. `zinc dev --lan` prints a QR code and a `zinc://<lan-ip>:<port>/<code>` link (a wrong code is refused); the player (`zn_app_player`: the interpreter, src/app/player.cpp, the swap code of src/dev/hot_swap.cpp) swaps a save in between two ticks with the existing Fast Refresh and restarts the program in the same app when a swap is impossible. zinc.json `"player": true` exports a player whose home screen ships as bytecode; examples/zinc-go is Zinc Go (scan with zinc:camera `scanQr()` on quirc 1.2 (ISC), type a link, reopen the last).
